Polity


- Chapter 1: Historical Background
    - Introduction
        - Overview
            - The British arrived in India in 1608 as traders under the East India Company, which was granted an exclusive trading charter by Queen Elizabeth I in 1600.
            - In 1765, following its victory in the Battle of Buxar (1764), the Company obtained the ‘diwani’ (rights over revenue and civil justice) of Bengal, Bihar, and Orissa from Mughal Emperor Shah Alam, marking its transition from a trading entity to a territorial power.
            - In 1858, after the Revolt of 1857, also known as the Sepoy Mutiny or First War of Independence, the British Crown assumed direct responsibility for governing India, a rule that continued until India gained independence on August 15, 1947.
            - With independence, the need for a Constitution arose, leading to the formation of a Constituent Assembly in 1946, which drafted the Constitution that came into effect on January 26, 1950.
            - Several features of the Indian Constitution and polity have their roots in British rule, shaped by significant events that established the legal framework for government and administration in British India.

    - The Company Rule (1773–1858)
        - Regulating Act of 1773
            - Significance
                - This act was a landmark in constitutional history as it represented the first step by the British Government to control and regulate the affairs of the East India Company in India.
                - It explicitly recognized the Company’s political and administrative functions for the first time.
                - It laid the foundations for a centralized administration in India.
            - Features
                - 1. Designated the Governor of Bengal as the ‘Governor-General of Bengal’ and established an Executive Council of four members to assist him. Lord Warren Hastings was appointed as the first Governor-General.
                - 2. Subordinated the Governors of Bombay and Madras presidencies to the Governor-General of Bengal, ending their previous independence.
                - 3. Established a Supreme Court at Calcutta in 1774, comprising one chief justice and three other judges.
                - 4. Prohibited Company servants from engaging in private trade or accepting presents or bribes from the ‘natives’ to curb corruption.
                - 5. Strengthened British Government oversight by requiring the Company’s Court of Directors to report on its revenue, civil, and military affairs in India.
        - Amending Act of 1781 (Act of Settlement)
            - Purpose
                - Enacted to address the shortcomings of the Regulating Act of 1773.
            - Features
                - 1. Exempted the Governor-General and his Council from the Supreme Court’s jurisdiction for actions taken in their official capacity, and similarly exempted Company servants for official acts.
                - 2. Excluded revenue matters and issues arising from revenue collection from the Supreme Court’s jurisdiction.
                - 3. Granted the Supreme Court jurisdiction over all inhabitants of Calcutta, requiring it to administer personal laws (Hindu law for Hindus, Mohammedan law for Muslims).
                - 4. Directed appeals from Provincial Courts to the Governor-General-in-Council instead of the Supreme Court.
                - 5. Empowered the Governor-General-in-Council to frame regulations for the Provincial Courts and Councils.
        - Pitt’s India Act of 1784
            - Introduction
                - Introduced by British Prime Minister William Pitt to further reform Company governance.
            - Features
                - 1. Distinguished between the Company’s commercial and political functions.
                - 2. Allowed the Court of Directors to manage commercial affairs but created a Board of Control to oversee political affairs, establishing a system of double government.
                - 3. Empowered the Board of Control to supervise and direct all civil, military, and revenue operations of the British possessions in India.
            - Significance
                - Marked the first time Company territories were referred to as ‘British possessions in India’.
                - Granted the British Government supreme control over the Company’s affairs and administration in India.
        - Act of 1786
            - Context
                - Enacted to meet the demands of Lord Cornwallis, appointed Governor-General of Bengal in 1786.
            - Features
                - 1. Granted Cornwallis the power to override his Council’s decisions in special cases.
                - 2. Appointed him as Commander-in-Chief alongside his role as Governor-General.
        - Charter Act of 1793
            - Features
                - 1. Extended the overriding power given to Lord Cornwallis to all future Governor-Generals and Governors of Presidencies.
                - 2. Enhanced the Governor-General’s control over the governments of Bombay and Madras presidencies.
                - 3. Extended the Company’s trade monopoly in India for another 20 years.
                - 4. Stipulated that the Commander-in-Chief would not be a member of the Governor-General’s Council unless specifically appointed.
                - 5. Provided that the Board of Control and its staff be paid from Indian revenues.
        - Charter Act of 1813
            - Features
                - 1. Abolished the Company’s trade monopoly in India, opening trade to all British merchants, except for tea and trade with China, which remained under Company control.
                - 2. Asserted the sovereignty of the British Crown over the Company’s territories in India.
                - 3. Permitted Christian missionaries to enter India to promote enlightenment among the population.
                - 4. Provided for the spread of Western education among inhabitants of British territories in India.
                - 5. Authorized local governments in India to impose taxes on persons and to punish non-payment.
        - Charter Act of 1833
            - Significance
                - Represented the final step toward centralization of administration in British India.
            - Features
                - 1. Redesignated the Governor-General of Bengal as the Governor-General of India, vesting him with all civil and military powers. Lord William Bentinck was the first Governor-General of India.
                - 2. Stripped the Governors of Bombay and Madras of their legislative powers, granting exclusive legislative authority to the Governor-General for all British India. Laws made under this act were called Acts, unlike earlier Regulations.
                - 3. Terminated the Company’s commercial activities, transforming it into a purely administrative body holding territories ‘in trust for His Majesty, His heirs and successors’.
                - 4. Attempted to introduce open competition for civil servant selection, stating that Indians should not be debarred from Company employment, though this provision was negated due to opposition from the Court of Directors.
        - Charter Act of 1853
            - Significance
                - The last Charter Act, marking a significant constitutional milestone.
            - Features
                - 1. Separated the legislative and executive functions of the Governor-General’s Council, adding six legislative councillors to form the Indian (Central) Legislative Council, which functioned as a mini-Parliament adopting British parliamentary procedures.
                - 2. Introduced an open competition system for selecting and recruiting civil servants, opening covenanted civil services to Indians. The Macaulay Committee on Indian Civil Service was appointed in 1854 to implement this.
                - 3. Extended the Company’s rule without specifying a duration, signaling that Parliament could terminate it at any time.
                - 4. Introduced local representation in the Indian (Central) Legislative Council, with four members appointed by the provincial governments of Madras, Bombay, Bengal, and Agra.

    - The Crown Rule (1858–1947)
        - Government of India Act of 1858
            - Context
                - Enacted in the wake of the Revolt of 1857, also known as the First War of Independence or Sepoy Mutiny, abolishing the East India Company and transferring governance, territories, and revenues to the British Crown.
            - Features
                - 1. Declared that India would henceforth be governed by and in the name of Her Majesty, changing the title of Governor-General to Viceroy of India. Lord Canning became the first Viceroy.
                - 2. Abolished the system of double government by eliminating the Board of Control and Court of Directors.
                - 3. Established the office of Secretary of State for India, vested with complete authority over Indian administration and responsible to the British Parliament.
                - 4. Created a 15-member Council of India to assist the Secretary of State, with the Secretary serving as Chairman.
                - 5. Constituted the Secretary of State-in-Council as a body corporate, capable of suing and being sued in India and England.
            - Note
                - The act primarily improved the administrative machinery for supervising and controlling the Indian Government from England, without significantly altering the system of governance in India (Subhash C. Kashyap, *Our Constitution*, National Book Trust, Third Edition, 2001, p. 14).
        - Indian Councils Act of 1861
            - Significance
                - A key landmark post-1857, aimed at associating Indians with administration following the revolt.
            - Features
                - 1. Initiated representative institutions by allowing the Viceroy to nominate Indians as non-official members to his expanded council. In 1862, Lord Canning nominated three Indians: the Raja of Benaras, the Maharaja of Patiala, and Sir Dinkar Rao.
                - 2. Restored legislative powers to the Bombay and Madras Presidencies, reversing the centralizing trend from the Regulating Act of 1773 and Charter Act of 1833, culminating in near-complete provincial autonomy by 1937.
                - 3. Provided for the establishment of new legislative councils for Bengal (1862), North-Western Provinces (1886), and Punjab (1897).
                - 4. Empowered the Viceroy to make rules for the convenient transaction of council business, recognizing the portfolio system introduced by Lord Canning in 1859, where council members managed specific departments.
                - 5. Authorized the Viceroy to issue ordinances without legislative council concurrence during emergencies, with a validity of six months.
        - Indian Councils Act of 1892
            - Features
                - 1. Increased the number of non-official members in the Central and provincial legislative councils, while maintaining an official majority.
                - 2. Expanded the functions of legislative councils, granting them the power to discuss the budget (introduced in British India in 1860) and address questions to the executive.
                - 3. Provided for the nomination of non-official members: (a) for the Central Legislative Council by the Viceroy on recommendations from provincial councils and the Bengal Chamber of Commerce; (b) for provincial councils by Governors on recommendations from district boards, municipalities, universities, trade associations, zamindars, and chambers.
            - Note
                - The act introduced a limited and indirect provision for elections to fill some non-official seats through recommendations, though the term ‘election’ was not used (V. N. Shukla, *The Constitution of India*, Eastern Book Company, Tenth Edition, 2001, p. A-10).
        - Indian Councils Act of 1909 (Morley-Minto Reforms)
            - Context
                - Named after Lord Morley (Secretary of State for India) and Lord Minto (Viceroy of India).
            - Features
                - 1. Significantly increased the size of legislative councils: the Central Legislative Council grew from 16 to 60 members, while provincial councils varied in size.
                - 2. Retained an official majority in the Central Legislative Council but allowed a non-official majority in provincial councils.
                - 3. Enlarged the deliberative functions of legislative councils, permitting members to ask supplementary questions, move resolutions on the budget, and engage in other discussions.
                - 4. Provided for the association of Indians with the executive councils of the Viceroy and Governors. Satyendra Prasad Sinha became the first Indian appointed as the Law Member of the Viceroy’s Executive Council.
                - 5. Introduced communal representation for Muslims through separate electorates, where Muslim members were elected solely by Muslim voters, legalizing communalism. Lord Minto earned the title Father of Communal Electorate.
                - 6. Provided separate representation for presidency corporations, chambers of commerce, universities, and zamindars.
        - Government of India Act of 1919 (Montagu-Chelmsford Reforms)
            - Context
                - Enacted in 1921, following the British declaration on August 20, 1917, committing to the gradual introduction of responsible government in India as an integral part of the British Empire.
            - Features
                - 1. Classified administrative subjects into central and provincial categories through Devolution Rules, relaxing central control and allowing both legislatures to make laws on their respective subjects, though the government remained centralized and unitary.
                - 2. Divided provincial subjects into transferred (e.g., public health, education, local self-government, agriculture) and reserved (e.g., police, justice, prisons, land revenue, finance), introducing dyarchy. Transferred subjects were administered by the Governor with ministers responsible to the legislative council, while reserved subjects were managed by the Governor and his executive council without legislative accountability. Dyarchy proved largely unsuccessful.
                - 3. Introduced bicameralism and direct elections, replacing the Indian Legislative Council with a bicameral legislature comprising an Upper House (Council of State) and a Lower House (Legislative Assembly), with most members elected directly.
                - 4. Required three of the six members of the Viceroy’s Executive Council (excluding the Commander-in-Chief) to be Indian.
                - 5. Extended communal representation by providing separate electorates for Sikhs, Indian Christians, Anglo-Indians, and Europeans.
                - 6. Granted franchise to a limited number of people based on property, tax, or other qualifications.
                - 7. Established the office of High Commissioner for India in London, transferring some functions from the Secretary of State for India.
                - 8. Provided for a Central Public Service Commission, established in 1926, following the Lee Commission’s recommendations (1923–24).
                - 9. Separated provincial budgets from the Central budget, authorizing provincial legislatures to enact their own budgets.
                - 10. Mandated a statutory commission to review the act’s functioning after ten years.
                - 11. Established the Chamber of Princes (Narendra Mandal), inaugurated in 1921, with 120 members (108 princes and 12 representatives of other states), headed by the Viceroy to discuss matters of common interest.
            - Simon Commission (1927)
                - Appointed in November 1927, two years ahead of schedule, under Sir John Simon to review the 1919 Act. Its all-British composition led to a boycott by Indian parties. The 1930 report recommended abolishing dyarchy, extending responsible government in provinces, establishing a federation of British India and princely states, and continuing communal electorates. Three round table conferences followed, resulting in a White Paper on Constitutional Reforms submitted to the Joint Select Committee of the British Parliament, which informed the Government of India Act of 1935.
            - Communal Award and Poona Pact (1932)
                - In August 1932, British Prime Minister Ramsay MacDonald announced the Communal Award, extending separate electorates to Muslims, Sikhs, Indian Christians, Anglo-Indians, Europeans, and depressed classes (Scheduled Castes).
                - Mahatma Gandhi, opposing separate electorates for depressed classes, undertook a fast unto death in Yerawada Jail (Poona), leading to the Poona Pact with Dr. B.R. Ambedkar. The pact retained Hindu joint electorates while reserving seats for depressed classes.
                - The British Government accepted the Poona Pact, modifying the Communal Award to eliminate separate electorates for depressed classes. The provisions of the Poona Pact were incorporated into the Government of India Act of 1935.
        - Government of India Act of 1935
            - Significance
                - Marked a second milestone toward a fully responsible government in India, comprising 321 sections and 10 schedules.
            - Features
                - 1. Provided for an All-India Federation of provinces and princely states, dividing powers into Federal List (59 items), Provincial List (54 items), and Concurrent List (36 items), with residuary powers vested in the Viceroy. The federation never materialized as princely states did not join.
                - 2. Abolished dyarchy in provinces, introducing provincial autonomy and responsible governments, effective from 1937 until discontinued in 1939.
                - 3. Introduced dyarchy at the Centre, dividing federal subjects into reserved and transferred, though this provision was never implemented.
                - 4. Established bicameralism in six of eleven provinces (Bengal, Bombay, Madras, Bihar, Assam, United Provinces), with legislative councils (upper house) and assemblies (lower house), subject to restrictions.
                - 5. Reserved general seats for depressed classes (Scheduled Castes) and extended special representation to women and labor (workers).
                - 6. Abolished the Council of India established by the 1858 Act, providing the Secretary of State with a team of advisors.
                - 7. Extended franchise to approximately 14% of the population.
                - 8. Established the Reserve Bank of India to control currency and credit.
                - 9. Provided for Federal, Provincial, and Joint Public Service Commissions for two or more provinces.
                - 10. Established a Federal Court, set up in 1937.
                - 11. Separated Burma (now Myanmar) from India.
                - 12. Created two new provinces: Orissa and Sind.
                - 13. Included safeguards to protect minority interests.
                - 14. Established a Federal Railway Authority to administer railways.
                - 15. Appointed an Auditor-General of India to audit federal and provincial accounts.
        - Indian Independence Act of 1947
            - Context
                - On February 20, 1947, British Prime Minister Clement Attlee announced the end of British rule by June 30, 1948, with power to be transferred to responsible Indian hands. The Muslim League’s demand for partition led to the Mountbatten Plan on June 3, 1947, accepted by Congress and the Muslim League. The Indian Independence Bill was introduced on July 4, 1947, received Royal Assent on July 18, 1947, and came into force on August 15, 1947.
            - Features
                - 1. Terminated British rule, declaring India an independent and sovereign state from August 15, 1947.
                - 2. Partitioned India into two independent dominions, India and Pakistan, with the right to secede from the British Commonwealth.
                - 3. Abolished the office of Viceroy, establishing Governor-General posts for each dominion, appointed by the British King on the advice of the dominion cabinet, with no British responsibility for their governance.
                - 4. Empowered the Constituent Assemblies of both dominions to frame and adopt their constitutions and repeal any British parliamentary act, including the Independence Act itself.
                - 5. Authorized the Constituent Assemblies to legislate for their territories until new constitutions were enforced, with no British Acts post-August 15, 1947, applying unless extended by dominion law.
                - 6. Abolished the office of Secretary of State for India, transferring its functions to the Secretary of State for Commonwealth Affairs.
                - 7. Ended British paramountcy over Indian princely states and treaty relations with tribal areas from August 15, 1947.
                - 8. Granted Indian princely states the freedom to join either the Dominion of India, the Dominion of Pakistan, or remain independent.
                - 9. Provided for governance of the dominions and provinces by the Government of India Act of 1935, with modifications, until new constitutions were framed.
                - 10. Removed the British Monarch’s right to veto bills or reserve them for approval, transferring this authority to the Governor-General, who could assent to bills in the name of His Majesty.
                - 11. Designated the Governor-General of India and provincial governors as constitutional (nominal) heads, required to act on the advice of their respective councils of ministers in all matters.
                - 12. Removed the title Emperor of India from the royal titles of the King of England.
                - 13. Discontinued appointments to civil services and reservation of posts by the Secretary of State for India, with members appointed before August 15, 1947, retaining their benefits.
            - Implementation
                - At midnight on August 14–15, 1947, British rule ended, and power was transferred to the Dominions of India and Pakistan. Lord Mountbatten became the first Governor-General of India, and Jawaharlal Nehru was sworn in as the first Prime Minister. The Constituent Assembly, formed in 1946, became the Parliament of the Indian Dominion.
                - A Boundary Commission, headed by Radcliffe, determined the boundaries. Pakistan included West Punjab, Sind, Baluchistan, East Bengal, North-Western Frontier Province, and Sylhet (Assam), based on referendums favoring Pakistan.

- Chapter 2: Making of the Constitution
    - Demand for a Constituent Assembly
        - Overview
            - The concept of a Constituent Assembly for India was first proposed in 1934 by M.N. Roy, a pioneer of the communist movement in India.
            - In 1935, the Indian National Congress (INC) officially demanded a Constituent Assembly to frame India’s Constitution.
            - In 1938, Jawaharlal Nehru, on behalf of the INC, declared that the Constitution of free India must be framed without external interference by a Constituent Assembly elected on the basis of adult franchise. This stance was reaffirmed by an INC resolution in 1939.
            - The British Government accepted the demand in principle through the ‘August Offer’ of 1940.
            - In 1942, Sir Stafford Cripps, a British Cabinet member, arrived in India with a draft proposal for an independent Constitution to be adopted post-World War II. The Cripps Proposals were rejected by the Muslim League, which demanded two autonomous states with separate Constituent Assemblies.
            - In 1946, a Cabinet Mission, comprising Lord Pethick-Lawrence, Sir Stafford Cripps, and A.V. Alexander, arrived in India on March 24 and published its plan on May 16. While rejecting the idea of two Constituent Assemblies, it proposed a scheme for a single Constituent Assembly that largely satisfied the Muslim League.

    - Composition of the Constituent Assembly
        - Overview
            - The Constituent Assembly was constituted in November 1946 under the Cabinet Mission Plan.
        - Features of the Scheme
            - 1. The total strength of the Constituent Assembly was set at 389 members, with 296 seats allotted to British India and 93 to princely states. Of the 296 British India seats, 292 were drawn from eleven governors’ provinces (Madras, Bombay, United Provinces, Bihar, Central Provinces, Orissa, Punjab, North-West Frontier Province, Sindh, Bengal, and Assam) and four from Chief Commissioners’ provinces (Delhi, Ajmer-Merwara, Coorg, and British Baluchistan), one per province.
            - 2. Seats for each province and princely state (or group of small states) were allocated proportionally to their population, roughly one seat per million people.
            - 3. Seats for British provinces were divided among three principal communities—Muslims, Sikhs, and General (all except Muslims and Sikhs)—based on their population.
            - 4. Community representatives were elected by members of their respective communities in provincial legislative assemblies using proportional representation with a single transferable vote.
            - 5. Representatives of princely states were nominated by the heads of the princely states.
        - Nature of the Assembly
            - The Constituent Assembly was a partly elected and partly nominated body, with members indirectly elected by provincial assemblies, which were themselves elected on a limited franchise under the Government of India Act of 1935, based on tax, property, and other qualifications.
        - Election Results
            - Elections for the 296 British India seats were held in July–August 1946. The INC won 208 seats, the Muslim League 73 seats, and small groups and independents secured the remaining 15 seats. The 93 princely state seats remained unfilled as they initially stayed away from the Assembly.
        - Representation
            - Despite not being directly elected on universal adult franchise, the Assembly included representatives from diverse sections of Indian society—Hindus, Muslims, Sikhs, Parsis, Anglo-Indians, Indian Christians, Scheduled Castes, Scheduled Tribes, and women from these groups. It featured prominent personalities of the time, except Mahatma Gandhi.

    - Working of the Constituent Assembly
        - Initial Meetings
            - The Constituent Assembly held its first meeting on December 9, 1946, attended by 207 members due to a Muslim League boycott demanding a separate Pakistan. Dr. Sachchidananda Sinha, the oldest member, was elected temporary Chairman, following French practice.
            - On December 11, 1946, Dr. Rajendra Prasad was elected permanent Chairman (President). On January 25, 1947, H.C. Mukherjee was elected Vice-President, and on July 16, 1947, V.T. Krishnamachari was elected as the second Vice-President.
        - Objectives Resolution
            - On December 13, 1946, Jawaharlal Nehru moved the ‘Objectives Resolution’, outlining the constitutional philosophy. It was unanimously adopted on January 22, 1947, and its modified version forms the Preamble of the Constitution. The resolution declared:
                - 1. India as an Independent Sovereign Republic with a Constitution for its governance.
                - 2. A Union comprising British India, Indian States, and other willing territories.
                - 3. Territories as autonomous units with residuary powers, except those vested in the Union.
                - 4. All power and authority derived from the people.
                - 5. Justice (social, economic, political), equality, and freedoms (thought, expression, belief, worship, etc.), subject to law and public morality.
                - 6. Safeguards for minorities, backward and tribal areas, and depressed classes.
                - 7. Territorial integrity and sovereign rights per international law.
                - 8. India’s contribution to world peace and mankind’s welfare.
        - Changes by the Indian Independence Act
            - On April 28, 1947, representatives from six princely states (Baroda, Bikaner, Jaipur, Patiala, Rewa, Udaipur) joined the Assembly. Following the Mountbatten Plan of June 3, 1947, for partition, most other princely state representatives and Muslim League members from the Indian Dominion joined.
            - The Indian Independence Act of 1947 made three changes:
                - 1. Declared the Assembly a fully sovereign body, empowered to frame any Constitution and abrogate British laws related to India.
                - 2. Made the Assembly a legislative body (Dominion Legislature), performing constitution-making and law-making on separate days, chaired by Dr. Rajendra Prasad for constitutional work and G.V. Mavalankar for legislative work (first meeting as Dominion Legislature on November 17, 1947). This dual role continued until November 26, 1949.
                - 3. Reduced the Assembly’s strength to 299 (from 389) after Muslim League members from Pakistan areas (West Punjab, East Bengal, North-West Frontier Province, Sindh, Baluchistan, Sylhet) withdrew. Indian provinces’ seats dropped from 296 to 229, and princely states’ from 93 to 70.
            - State-wise membership as of December 31, 1947, is detailed in:
                - ------------------------------------------------- Table 2.5 -------------------------------------------------
        - Other Functions Performed
            - Beyond constitution-making and legislation, the Assembly:
                - 1. Ratified India’s Commonwealth membership in May 1949.
                - 2. Adopted the national flag on July 22, 1947.
                - 3. Adopted the national anthem on January 24, 1950.
                - 4. Adopted the national song on January 24, 1950.
                - 5. Elected Dr. Rajendra Prasad as India’s first President on January 24, 1950.
        - Duration and Sessions
            - The Assembly held 11 sessions over 2 years, 11 months, and 18 days, studying constitutions of about 60 countries. The Draft Constitution was considered for 114 days, with a total expenditure of ₹64 lakh.
            - The final session was on January 24, 1950, but the Assembly continued as the provisional parliament from January 26, 1950, until the first elected Parliament formed in May 1952 after the 1951–52 elections.

    - Committees of the Constituent Assembly
        - Overview
            - The Assembly appointed several committees, including eight major and several minor ones, to handle constitution-making tasks.
        - Major Committees
            - 1. Union Powers Committee – Jawaharlal Nehru
            - 2. Union Constitution Committee – Jawaharlal Nehru
            - 3. Provincial Constitution Committee – Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel
            - 4. Drafting Committee – Dr. B.R. Ambedkar
            - 5. Advisory Committee on Fundamental Rights, Minorities, and Tribal and Excluded Areas – Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel, with sub-committees:
                - (a) Fundamental Rights Sub-Committee – J.B. Kripalani
                - (b) Minorities Sub-Committee – H.C. Mukherjee
                - (c) North-East Frontier Tribal Areas and Assam Excluded & Partially Excluded Areas Sub-Committee – Gopinath Bardoloi
                - (d) Excluded and Partially Excluded Areas (Other than Assam) Sub-Committee – A.V. Thakkar
                - (e) North-West Frontier Tribal Areas Sub-Committee – No Chairman (did not function as the area became part of Pakistan post-June 3, 1947; members: Khan Abdul Ghaffar Khan, Khan Abdul Samad Khan, Mehr Chand Khanna).
            - 6. Rules of Procedure Committee – Dr. Rajendra Prasad
            - 7. States Committee (Negotiating with States) – Jawaharlal Nehru
            - 8. Steering Committee – Dr. Rajendra Prasad
        - Minor Committees
            - 1. Finance and Staff Committee – Dr. Rajendra Prasad
            - 2. Credentials Committee – Alladi Krishnaswami Ayyar
            - 3. House Committee – B. Pattabhi Sitaramayya
            - 4. Order of Business Committee – Dr. K.M. Munshi
            - 5. Ad-hoc Committee on the National Flag – Dr. Rajendra Prasad
            - 6. Committee on the Functions of the Constituent Assembly – G.V. Mavalankar
            - 7. Ad-hoc Committee on the Supreme Court – S. Varadachari (Non-member)
            - 8. Committee on Chief Commissioners’ Provinces – B. Pattabhi Sitaramayya
            - 9. Expert Committee on Financial Provisions – Nalini Ranjan Sarkar (Non-member)
            - 10. Linguistic Provinces Commission – S.K. Dar (Non-member)
            - 11. Special Committee to Examine the Draft Constitution – Jawaharlal Nehru
            - 12. Press Gallery Committee – Usha Nath Sen
            - 13. Ad-hoc Committee on Citizenship – S. Varadachari (Non-member)
        - Drafting Committee
            - Established on August 29, 1947, as the most critical committee, tasked with preparing the Constitution’s draft. Members:
                - 1. Dr. B.R. Ambedkar (Chairman)
                - 2. N. Gopalaswami Ayyangar
                - 3. Alladi Krishnaswami Ayyar
                - 4. Dr. K.M. Munshi
                - 5. Syed Mohammad Saadullah
                - 6. N. Madhava Rau (replaced B.L. Mitter due to ill-health)
                - 7. T.T. Krishnamachari (replaced D.P. Khaitan after his death in 1948)
            - The committee published the first draft in February 1948, allowing eight months for public discussion. After incorporating feedback, a second draft was published in October 1948. The committee completed its work in under six months, sitting for 141 days.

    - Enactment of the Constitution
        - Process
            - Dr. B.R. Ambedkar introduced the final draft on November 4, 1948, for the first reading, followed by a five-day general discussion until November 9, 1948.
            - The second reading (clause-by-clause) ran from November 15, 1948, to October 17, 1949, with 7,653 amendments proposed and 2,473 discussed.
            - The third reading began on November 14, 1949. Dr. Ambedkar moved the motion to pass the Constitution, which was adopted on November 26, 1949, with 284 of 299 members present signing it.
        - Details
            - The Constitution, as adopted, contained a Preamble, 395 Articles, and 8 Schedules. The Preamble was enacted after the rest of the Constitution.
            - Dr. Ambedkar, as Law Minister, played a pivotal role, earning recognition as the ‘Father’ and ‘Chief Architect’ of the Constitution for his logical and persuasive arguments.
            - Since November 19, 2015, November 26 is celebrated as “Constitution Day” (Samvidhan Divas) to promote constitutional values.

    - Enforcement of the Constitution
        - Timeline
            - Provisions related to citizenship, elections, provisional parliament, temporary/transitional provisions, and short title (Articles 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 60, 324, 366, 367, 379, 380, 388, 391, 392, 393) came into force on November 26, 1949.
            - The remaining provisions took effect on January 26, 1950, known as the ‘date of commencement’ and celebrated as Republic Day.
        - Significance
            - January 26 was chosen due to its historical importance, marking Purna Swaraj Day in 1930, following the INC’s Lahore Session resolution in December 1929.
        - Repeals
            - The Indian Independence Act of 1947 and the Government of India Act of 1935 (with amendments) were repealed. The Abolition of Privy Council Jurisdiction Act (1949) continued.
