    - Experts Committee of the Congress
        - Overview
            - On July 8, 1946, while Constituent Assembly elections were ongoing, the INC formed an Experts Committee to prepare material for the Assembly. Members:
                - 1. Jawaharlal Nehru (Chairman)
                - 2. M. Asaf Ali
                - 3. K.M. Munshi
                - 4. N. Gopalaswami Ayyangar
                - 5. K.T. Shah
                - 6. D.R. Gadgil
                - 7. Humayun Kabir
                - 8. K. Santhanam
                - Krishna Kripalani (co-opted as member and convener).
        - Meetings and Role
            - The committee held two sittings: July 20–22, 1946, in New Delhi, and August 15–17, 1946, in Bombay.
            - It discussed the Assembly’s procedure, committee appointments, and drafted an objectives resolution. Granville Austin noted that this committee set India on the path to its Constitution, making key suggestions within the Cabinet Mission framework.
    
    - Criticism of the Constituent Assembly
        - Grounds of Criticism
            - 1. **Not a Representative Body**: Critics argued the Assembly was not representative as members were not directly elected on universal adult franchise but indirectly by provincial assemblies.
            - 2. **Not a Sovereign Body**: Critics claimed it lacked sovereignty, being created by British proposals and operating with British permission.
            - 3. **Time Consuming**: The Assembly took nearly three years, criticized as excessive compared to the U.S. Constitution’s four months. Naziruddin Ahmed mockingly called the Drafting Committee a “Drifting Committee”. Time taken by other constitutions is in:
                - ------------------------------------------------- Table 2.7 -------------------------------------------------
            - 4. **Dominated by Congress**: Critics, including Granville Austin, noted Congress dominance, describing the Assembly as a one-party body in a largely one-party country.
            - 5. **Lawyer-Politician Domination**: The Assembly was criticized for being dominated by lawyers and politicians, with under-representation of other societal sections, leading to a bulky and complex Constitution.
            - 6. **Dominated by Hindus**: Critics like Lord Viscount Simon and Winston Churchill called it a Hindu-dominated body, representing primarily one community.

    - Important Facts
        - 1. The elephant was the symbol (seal) of the Constituent Assembly.
        - 2. Sir B.N. Rau was the constitutional advisor (Legal Advisor).
        - 3. H.V.R. Iyengar was the Secretary to the Assembly.
        - 4. S.N. Mukherjee was the chief draftsman.
        - 5. Prem Behari Narain Raizada was the calligrapher, handwriting the original Constitution in italic style.
        - 6. Artists from Shantiniketan, including Nandalal Bose and Beohar Rammanohar Sinha, beautified the original Constitution.
        - 7. Beohar Rammanohar Sinha illuminated the Preamble.
        - 8. Vasant Krishan Vaidya calligraphed the Hindi version, decorated by Nandalal Bose.

    - Hindi Text of the Constitution
        - Overview
            - Initially, the Constitution had no provision for an authoritative Hindi text. The 58th Constitutional Amendment Act of 1987 added Article 394-A in Part XXII, providing:
                - 1. The President shall publish a Hindi translation of the Constitution, incorporating all amendments and adapting language, style, and terminology to align with Central Acts in Hindi; and a Hindi translation of every amendment.
                - 2. The Hindi translation shall have the same meaning as the English original, with the President revising it if difficulties arise.
                - 3. The Hindi translation is deemed the authoritative text in Hindi for all purposes.


- Chapter 3: Concept of the Constitution
    - Meaning
        - Overview
            - The term 'constitution' originates from the Latin word constituere, meaning 'to establish' or 'to set up'.
            - In its modern context, a constitution refers to a set of fundamental principles that define the organization and functioning of the government, as well as the relationship between the government and the people, outlining their rights and duties.
            - A constitution is described by various terms, such as the 'fundamental law of the land', 'supreme law of the state', 'basic law of the country', 'instrument of the government', 'rules of the state', 'basic structure of the polity', and 'grundnorm of the country'.
        - Definitions by Scholars
            - Gilchrist: "The constitution consists of that body of rules or laws which determine the organization of government, the distribution of powers to the various organs of government, and the general principles on which these powers are to be exercised."
                - Citation: Gilchrist, R.N., Principles of Political Science, 1961, p. 211.
            - Gettell: "The fundamental principles that determine the form of a state are called its constitution. These include the method by which the state is organised, the distribution of its sovereign powers among the various organs of government, the scope and manner of exercise of governmental functions, and the relation of the government to the people over whom its authority is exercised."
                - Citation: Gettell, R.G., Political Science, 1956, p. 244.
            - Wheare: "The Constitution describes the whole system of government of a country, the collection of rules which establish and regulate or govern the government."
                - Citation: Wheare, K.C., Modern Constitutions, 1956, p. 1.
            - Wade and Phillips: "A constitution is a document having a special legal sanctity which sets out the framework and the principal functions of the organs of the Government of a State and declares the principles governing the operation of those organs."
                - Citation: Wade and Phillips, Constitutional Law, 1965, p. 1.

    - Functions
        - Overview
            - Constitutional scholar Elliot Bulmer comprehensively identifies the functions (or purposes) of a constitution, which include:
                - 1. Declaring and defining the boundaries of the political community.
                - 2. Declaring and defining the nature and authority of the political community.
                - 3. Expressing the identity and values of a national community.
                - 4. Declaring and defining the rights and duties of citizens.
                - 5. Establishing and regulating the political institutions of the community.
                - 6. Dividing or sharing power between different layers of government or sub-state communities.
                - 7. Declaring the official religious identity of the state and demarcating relationships between sacred and secular authorities.
                - 8. Committing states to particular social, economic, or developmental goals.
            - Citation: Elliot Bulmer, What is a Constitution? Principles and Concepts, International IDEA Constitution-Building Primer 1, Second Edition, 2017, International Institute for Democracy and Electoral Assistance, pp. 6-7.

    - Qualities
        - Overview
            - A good constitution possesses certain qualities or characteristics, which are essential for its effectiveness. These include:
                - 1. Brevity: A constitution should be concise, avoiding unnecessary provisions. Excessive detail can lead to confusion in interpreting its clauses.
                - 2. Clarity: Provisions should be expressed in clear, understandable terms to enhance comprehension and reduce ambiguity.
                - 3. Definiteness: Provisions should have precise meanings to minimize judicial discretion and ensure consistent interpretation.
                - 4. Comprehensiveness: A constitution should thoroughly outline the government's powers and the citizens' rights and duties, reducing scope for disputes and litigation.
                - 5. Suitability: It must reflect the historical, socio-cultural, economic, and political conditions of the nation, aligning with the people's needs and aspirations.
                - 6. Stability: A constitution should promote political stability by resisting easy amendments, fostering citizens' respect and obedience.
                - 7. Adaptability: It should be dynamic, capable of adapting to changing circumstances, making it a living document.

    - Classification
        - Overview
            - Constitutions are classified into various types based on different criteria, as follows:
        - 1. Evolved and Enacted
            - Based on their origin, constitutions are classified as evolved or enacted.
            - An evolved constitution develops gradually through conventions, practices, principles, and judicial decisions. It is also called a cumulative constitution. The British Constitution is a prime example.
            - An enacted constitution is deliberately created by a constituent assembly, constitutional council, or promulgated by a king or parliament. It is documented in a book or series of documents and is known as a conventional constitution. The American and Indian Constitutions are examples.
        - 2. Written and Unwritten
            - Based on the incorporation of provisions, constitutions are classified as written or unwritten.
            - A written constitution is documented in a book, document, or series of documents, consciously formulated by a constituent assembly or constitutional convention. It is also known as a documentary or codified constitution. Examples include the constitutions of the USA, Canada, Japan, France, and India.
            - An unwritten constitution lacks a single documented form, existing as conventions, practices, principles, charters, statutes, and judicial decisions. It evolves historically and is known as a non-documentary or uncodified constitution. The constitutions of the UK, New Zealand, and Israel are examples.
            - The distinction between written and unwritten constitutions largely aligns with that between enacted and evolved constitutions.
        - 3. Rigid and Flexible
            - Based on amendment procedures, constitutions are classified as rigid or flexible, a classification introduced by Lord James Bryce.
                - Citation: Bryce, J., Studies in History and Jurisprudence, Volume 1, p. 127.
            - A rigid constitution requires a special procedure for amendments, distinct from ordinary laws, emphasizing the supremacy of constitutional law. Examples include the constitutions of the USA, Australia, and Switzerland.
            - A flexible constitution can be amended like ordinary laws, without a special procedure. It is also called an elastic constitution. The constitutions of the UK and New Zealand are examples.
            - The Constitution of India is a synthesis of both rigid and flexible elements.
        - 4. Federal and Unitary
            - Based on the relationship between national and regional governments, constitutions are classified as federal or unitary.
            - A federal constitution divides power between national and regional governments, allowing independent operation within their jurisdictions. Examples include the USA, Switzerland, Australia, Canada, Russia, and Brazil.
            - A unitary constitution centralizes power in the national government, with regional governments acting as subordinates, exercising delegated powers. Examples include the UK, France, Japan, China, Italy, and Norway.
            - The Constitution of India combines federal and unitary features, described as quasi-federal by K.C. Wheare.
        - 5. Procedural and Prescriptive
            - According to Elliot Bulmer, constitutions can be procedural or prescriptive, based on their nature and purpose.
                - Citation: Elliot Bulmer, What is a Constitution? Principles and Concepts, International IDEA Constitution-Building Primer 1, Second Edition, 2017, pp. 9-10.
            - A procedural constitution defines the legal and political structures of public institutions and sets legal limits on government power to protect democratic processes and fundamental human rights.
            - A prescriptive constitution, in addition to outlining government functions, establishes a consensus on societal goals that public authorities must pursue.

    - Constitutionalism and Constitutional Government
        - Meaning
            - While closely related, the concepts of constitution and constitutionalism differ in key ways:
                - 1. A country may have a constitution but not constitutionalism. For example, a dictatorship with a constitution based on the dictator's will lacks constitutionalism.
                    - Citation: Jain, M.P., Indian Constitutional Law, Eighth Edition, Lexis Nexis, Gurgaon, 2018, p. 6.
                - 2. Constitutionalism recognizes the need for government power but insists on limitations to prevent authoritarianism. Only a constitution that restricts governmental power embodies constitutionalism.
                    - Citation: Ibid.
                - 3. Constitutionalism envisions a polity governed by a constitution that ensures limited government and the rule of law, opposing arbitrary, despotic, or totalitarian rule. Constitutional government is inherently democratic, and arbitrary power negates constitutionalism.
                    - Citation: Kashyap, Dr. Subhash C., Constitutional Law of India, Second Edition, Volume 1, Universal Law Publishing, Gurgaon, 2015, p. 7.
                - 4. Constitutionalism seeks a political order with limited government power, synonymous with a civilized government. The justification for a constitution lies in maintaining a limited government accountable to laws and rules.
                    - Citation: Johari, J.C., Comparative Politics, Fourth Edition, Sterling Publishers Private Limited, New Delhi, 2011, p. 217.
        - Definitions
            - Friedrich: "Constitutionalism provides a system of effective restraints upon governmental action. It is a body of rules ensuring fair play, thus rendering the government responsible."
                - Citation: Friedrich, Carl J., Constitutional Government and Democracy, Oxford and IBH, Calcutta, 1964, p. 25.
            - Roucek: "Constitutionalism means essentially limited government. It is the antithesis of government by the unrestrained will of rulers. Regardless of the actual form of effectiveness of restraints, it assumes limitations on government as opposed to arbitrary government."
                - Citation: Roucek, J.S. and Others, Introduction to Political Science, 1954, p. 981.
            - Wheare: "Constitutional government means something more than a government according to the terms of a constitution. It means government according to rule as opposed to arbitrary government; it means government limited by the terms of a constitution, not government limited only by the desires and capacities of those who exercise power."
                - Citation: Wheare, K.C., Modern Constitutions, University Press, London, 1956, p. 137.
            - Ybema: "A form of government can only be classified as constitutional when the rulers are subject to a body of rules and principles, which limit the exercise of their power. Constitutional government is the antithesis of arbitrary rule."
                - Citation: Ybema, S.P., Constitutionalism and Civil Liberties, Leiden University Press, Leiden, 1973, p. 1.
        - Elements
            - Constitutional scholar Louis Henkin identifies eight elements (features or principles) of constitutionalism:
                - 1. Popular sovereignty
                - 2. Rule of law
                - 3. Democratic government (responsible and accountable government)
                - 4. Separation of powers (checks and balances)
                - 5. An independent judiciary
                - 6. Civilian control of the military
                - 7. Police governed by law and judicial control
                - 8. Respect for individual rights
            - Citation: Henkin, Louis, A New Birth of Constitutionalism: Genetic Influences and Genetic Defects, Chapter 2 in Constitutionalism, Identity, Difference and Legitimacy: Theoretical Perspectives, Edited by Michel Rosenfeld, Duke University Press, Durham, 1994, pp. 39-54.


- Chapter 4: Salient Features of the Constitution
    - Overview
        - The Indian Constitution is unique in its contents and spirit, distinguished by several salient features that set it apart from other constitutions worldwide, despite borrowing from various global constitutions.
        - Many original features of the Constitution (as adopted in 1949) have changed due to amendments, notably the 7th, 42nd, 44th, 73rd, 74th, 97th, and 101st Amendments. The 42nd Amendment Act (1976), known as the 'Mini-Constitution', introduced extensive changes.
        - In the Kesavananda Bharati case (Kesavananda Bharati vs. State of Kerala, 1973), the Supreme Court ruled that Parliament’s constituent power under Article 368 cannot alter the 'basic structure' of the Constitution.

    - Salient Features of the Constitution
        - Overview
            - The salient features of the Constitution, as it stands today, are as follows:
        - 1. Lengthiest Written Constitution
            - Constitutions are classified as written (e.g., American Constitution) or unwritten (e.g., British Constitution). The Indian Constitution is the lengthiest written constitution globally, characterized as comprehensive, elaborate, and detailed.
            - Originally (1949), it contained a Preamble, 395 Articles (in 22 Parts), and 8 Schedules. Presently, it includes a Preamble, about 470 Articles (in 25 Parts), and 12 Schedules. Amendments since 1951 have deleted about 20 Articles and Part VII, adding about 95 Articles, four Parts (IVA, IXA, IXB, XIVA), and four Schedules (9, 10, 11, 12).
            - Comparatively, the American Constitution had 7 Articles, Australian 128, Chinese 138, and Canadian 147.
            - The Parts, Important Articles, and Schedules are detailed in:
                - ------------------------------------------------- Table 4.1 -------------------------------------------------
                - ------------------------------------------------- Table 4.2 -------------------------------------------------
                - ------------------------------------------------- Table 4.3 -------------------------------------------------
            - Four factors contributed to its size:
                - (a) Geographical factors, such as the country’s vastness and diversity.
                - (b) Historical factors, notably the influence of the bulky Government of India Act, 1935.
                - (c) A single Constitution for both Centre and states.
                - (d) Dominance of legal luminaries in the Constituent Assembly.
            - The Constitution includes fundamental governance principles and detailed administrative provisions, unlike other democracies where such matters are left to ordinary legislation or conventions.
            - Until 2019, Jammu and Kashmir had its own constitution under Article 370, granting special status. This was abolished by the Constitution (Application to Jammu and Kashmir) Order, 2019, superseding the 1954 order, extending all constitutional provisions to Jammu and Kashmir. The Jammu and Kashmir Reorganisation Act, 2019, bifurcated it into two Union territories: Jammu & Kashmir and Ladakh.
        - 2. Drawn from Various Sources
            - The Constitution borrows extensively from other constitutions and the Government of India Act, 1935, with about 250 provisions from the latter included.
            - Dr. B.R. Ambedkar noted it was framed after 'ransacking all the known Constitutions of the World' (Constituent Assembly Debates, Volume VII, pp. 35–38).
            - The structural part derives from the Government of India Act, 1935; the philosophical part (Fundamental Rights, Directive Principles) from American and Irish Constitutions; and the political part (Cabinet Government, Executive-Legislature relations) from the British Constitution (P.M. Bakshi, The Constitution of India, Universal, Fifth Edition, 2002, p. 4).
            - Other provisions come from Canada, Australia, Germany, Soviet Union (now Russia), France, South Africa, Japan, etc.
            - The Government of India Act, 1935, is the most significant source, contributing the Federal Scheme, Judiciary, Governors, Emergency Powers, Public Service Commissions, and administrative details (Brij Kishore Sharma, Introduction to the Constitution of India, Seventh Edition, 2015, p. 42).
            - Borrowed features are listed in:
                - ------------------------------------------------- Table 4.4 -------------------------------------------------
        - 3. Blend of Rigidity and Flexibility
            - Constitutions are classified as rigid (e.g., American, requiring special amendment procedures) or flexible (e.g., British, amended like ordinary laws). The Indian Constitution is a synthesis of both.
            - Article 368 provides two amendment types:
                - (a) Some provisions require a special majority (two-thirds of members present and voting in each House, plus a majority of total membership).
                - (b) Others require a special majority plus ratification by half of the states.
            - Certain provisions can be amended by a simple majority, not falling under Article 368.
        - 4. Federal System with Unitary Bias
            - The Constitution establishes a federal system with features like two governments, division of powers, written Constitution, supremacy of Constitution, rigidity, independent judiciary, and bicameralism.
            - It also includes unitary features: strong Centre, single Constitution, single citizenship, flexibility, integrated judiciary, Centre-appointed governors, All-India Services, emergency provisions, etc.
            - The term 'Federation' is absent; Article 1 describes India as a 'Union of States', implying no state agreement or right to secede.
            - Scholars describe it as 'federal in form but unitary in spirit', 'quasi-federal' (K.C. Wheare), 'bargaining federalism' (Morris Jones), 'co-operative federalism' (Granville Austin), or 'federation with a centralising tendency' (Ivor Jennings).
        - 5. Parliamentary Form of Government
            - The Constitution adopts the British Parliamentary System over the American Presidential System, emphasizing cooperation between legislative and executive organs, unlike the presidential system’s separation of powers.
            - Known as the 'Westminster' Model, responsible government, or Cabinet government, it operates at both Centre and states.
            - Features include:
                - (a) Nominal and real executives.
                - (b) Majority party rule.
                - (c) Collective responsibility of the executive to the legislature.
                - (d) Ministers’ membership in the legislature.
                - (e) Leadership of the Prime Minister or Chief Minister.
                - (f) Dissolution of the lower House (Lok Sabha or Assembly).
            - Differences from the British system include the Indian Parliament’s lack of sovereignty and India’s elected head (republic) versus Britain’s hereditary head (monarchy).
            - The Prime Minister’s role is so significant that it is often called a 'Prime Ministerial Government'.
        - 6. Synthesis of Parliamentary Sovereignty and Judicial Supremacy
            - Parliamentary sovereignty is associated with the British Parliament, judicial supremacy with the American Supreme Court.
            - India’s judicial review power is narrower than in the USA due to 'procedure established by law' (Article 21) versus 'due process of law'.
            - The Constitution balances parliamentary sovereignty (Parliament’s amendment power) with judicial supremacy (Supreme Court’s power to declare laws unconstitutional).
        - 7. Integrated and Independent Judiciary
            - The Constitution establishes an integrated and independent judicial system, with the Supreme Court at the apex, followed by state high courts and subordinate courts (district and lower courts).
            - This single system enforces both central and state laws, unlike the USA’s dual judiciary.
            - The Supreme Court is a federal court, highest appellate court, guarantor of fundamental rights, and guardian of the Constitution.
            - Independence is ensured through security of tenure, fixed service conditions, expenses charged on the Consolidated Fund, prohibition on discussing judges’ conduct in legislatures, ban on post-retirement practice, contempt powers, and separation from the executive.
        - 8. Fundamental Rights
            - Part III guarantees six fundamental rights:
                - (a) Right to Equality (Articles 14–18).
                - (b) Right to Freedom (Articles 19–22).
                - (c) Right against Exploitation (Articles 23–24).
                - (d) Right to Freedom of Religion (Articles 25–28).
                - (e) Cultural and Educational Rights (Articles 29–30).
                - (f) Right to Constitutional Remedies (Article 32).
            - Originally, seven rights were included, but the Right to Property (Article 31) was removed by the 44th Amendment Act (1978) and made a legal right under Article 300-A.
            - Fundamental Rights promote political democracy, limit executive tyranny, and are justiciable, enforceable via writs (habeas corpus, mandamus, prohibition, certiorari, quo warranto).
            - They are subject to reasonable restrictions, can be amended or repealed, and may be suspended during a National Emergency (except Articles 20 and 21).
        - 9. Directive Principles of State Policy
            - Described by Dr. B.R. Ambedkar as a 'novel feature', these are enumerated in Part IV, categorized as socialistic, Gandhian, and liberal-intellectual.
            - They aim to establish social and economic democracy and a welfare state but are non-justiciable.
            - The Constitution declares them fundamental to governance, imposing a moral obligation on the state, with public opinion as their sanction.
            - In the Minerva Mills case (1980), the Supreme Court emphasized the balance between Fundamental Rights and Directive Principles.
        - 10. Fundamental Duties
            - Added by the 42nd Amendment Act (1976) during the 1975–77 emergency, based on the Swaran Singh Committee’s recommendations, with one more duty added by the 86th Amendment Act (2002).
            - Part IV-A (Article 51-A) lists eleven duties, including respecting the Constitution, national flag, and anthem; protecting sovereignty; promoting brotherhood; and preserving cultural heritage.
            - They remind citizens of their obligations but are non-justiciable.
        - 11. A Secular State
            - The Constitution establishes a secular state, not upholding any official religion. Key provisions include:
                - (a) 'Secular' added to the Preamble by the 42nd Amendment Act (1976).
                - (b) Liberty of belief, faith, and worship (Preamble).
                - (c) Equality before the law (Article 14).
                - (d) Non-discrimination on religious grounds (Article 15).
                - (e) Equal opportunity in public employment (Article 16).
                - (f) Freedom of conscience and religion (Article 25).
                - (g) Right to manage religious affairs (Article 26).
                - (h) No taxes for promoting a religion (Article 27).
                - (i) No religious instruction in state institutions (Article 28).
                - (j) Right to conserve language, script, or culture (Article 29).
                - (k) Minority rights to educational institutions (Article 30).
                - (l) Endeavour for a Uniform Civil Code (Article 44).
            - Indian secularism adopts a positive approach (equal respect for all religions) rather than the Western model of complete separation.
            - Communal representation (as in 1909, 1919, 1935 Acts) was abolished, but temporary reservations exist for Scheduled Castes and Tribes.
        - 12. Universal Adult Franchise
            - The Constitution adopts universal adult franchise for elections to Lok Sabha and state assemblies, granting voting rights to citizens aged 18 or above without discrimination.
            - The voting age was reduced from 21 to 18 by the 61st Amendment Act (1988).
            - This was a bold experiment given India’s size, population, poverty, inequality, and illiteracy, unlike gradual franchise extensions in Western countries (e.g., USA in 1920, UK in 1928).
            - It broadens democracy, enhances equality, and empowers minorities and weaker sections.
        - 13. Single Citizenship
            - Despite a federal structure, the Constitution provides only Indian citizenship, unlike the USA’s dual citizenship.
            - All citizens enjoy uniform political and civil rights nationwide, without state-based discrimination.
            - Challenges like communal riots and ethnic disputes indicate incomplete realization of national unity.
        - 14. Independent Bodies
            - The Constitution establishes independent bodies to support democracy:
                - (a) Election Commission for free and fair elections.
                - (b) Comptroller and Auditor-General to audit government accounts.
                - (c) Union Public Service Commission for All-India and higher Central services recruitment.
                - (d) State Public Service Commissions for state services recruitment.
            - Independence is ensured through security of tenure, fixed conditions, and expenses charged on the Consolidated Fund.
        - 15. Emergency Provisions
            - The Constitution includes emergency provisions to address extraordinary situations, safeguarding sovereignty, unity, and democracy.
            - Three types are:
                - (a) National Emergency (Article 352) for war, external aggression, or armed rebellion (replacing 'internal disturbance' per 44th Amendment, 1978).
                - (b) State Emergency (President’s Rule, Articles 356, 365) for constitutional machinery failure or non-compliance with Centre’s directions.
                - (c) Financial Emergency (Article 360) for financial stability threats.
            - During emergencies, the Centre gains total control, converting the federal structure to unitary without formal amendment.
        - 16. Three-tier Government
            - Originally dual (Centre and states), the Constitution added a third tier via the 73rd and 74th Amendment Acts (1992).
            - The 73rd Amendment recognized panchayats (Part IX, Schedule 11), establishing a three-tier system (village, intermediate, district).
            - The 74th Amendment recognized municipalities (Part IX-A, Schedule 12), with nagar panchayats, municipal councils, and corporations.
        - 17. Co-operative Societies
            - The 97th Amendment Act (2011) granted constitutional status to co-operative societies through:
                - 1. Right to form co-operative societies as a fundamental right (Article 19).
                - 2. Directive Principle on co-operative societies (Article 43-B).
                - 3. New Part IX-B (Articles 243-ZH to 243-ZT) for co-operative societies.
            - Part IX-B ensures democratic, professional, and autonomous functioning, empowering Parliament and state legislatures to legislate.

    - Criticism of the Constitution
        - Overview
            - The Constitution has faced criticism on several grounds:
        - 1. A Borrowed Constitution
            - Critics called it a 'borrowed Constitution', 'bag of borrowings', or 'patchwork', arguing it lacks originality.
            - Dr. B.R. Ambedkar countered that constitutions globally share fundamentals, and India’s framers adapted borrowed features to suit Indian needs, avoiding faults (Constituent Assembly Debates, Volume VII, pp. 35–38).
        - 2. A Carbon Copy of the 1935 Act
            - Critics noted extensive incorporation of the Government of India Act, 1935, calling it a 'carbon copy' or 'amended version' (N. Srinivasan, Ivor Jennings, P.R. Deshmukh).
            - Ambedkar defended borrowing administrative details, emphasizing no shame in adopting proven provisions (Ibid).
        - 3. Un-Indian or Anti-Indian
            - Critics argued it is 'un-Indian' or 'anti-Indian', not reflecting India’s political traditions (K. Hanumanthaiyya, Lokanath Misra, Lakshminarayan Sahu), citing its Western influence.
        - 4. An Un-Gandhian Constitution
            - Critics claimed it lacks Gandhian philosophy, particularly village-based governance (K. Hanumanthaiyya, T. Prakasam).
        - 5. Elephantine Size
            - Critics noted its bulkiness and complexity (Ivor Jennings, H.V. Kamath), comparing it to the Assembly’s elephant emblem.
        - 6. Paradise of the Lawyers
            - Critics called it overly legalistic, a 'lawyer’s paradise' (Ivor Jennings, H.K. Maheshwari, P.R. Deshmukh), arguing its complexity encourages litigation.


- Chapter 5: Preamble of the Constitution

    - Overview
        - The American Constitution was the first to begin with a Preamble, a practice followed by many countries, including India. The term 'Preamble' refers to the introduction or preface to the Constitution, encapsulating its summary or essence.
        - N.A. Palkhivala, an eminent jurist and constitutional expert, described the Preamble as the 'identity card of the Constitution'.
        - The Preamble to the Indian Constitution is based on the 'Objectives Resolution', drafted and moved by Pandit Nehru on December 13, 1946, and adopted by the Constituent Assembly on January 22, 1947.
        - It was amended by the 42nd Constitutional Amendment Act (1976), which added three new words: Socialist, Secular, and Integrity.

    - Text of the Preamble
        - The Preamble in its present form reads:
            - WE, THE PEOPLE OF INDIA, having solemnly resolved to constitute India into a SOVEREIGN SOCIALIST SECULAR DEMOCRATIC REPUBLIC and to secure to all its citizens:
                - JUSTICE, Social, Economic and Political;
                - LIBERTY of thought, expression, belief, faith and worship;
                - EQUALITY of status and of opportunity; and to promote among them all;
                - FRATERNITY assuring the dignity of the individual and the unity and integrity of the Nation;
            - IN OUR CONSTITUENT ASSEMBLY this twenty-sixth day of November, 1949, do HEREBY ADOPT, ENACT AND GIVE TO OURSELVES THIS CONSTITUTION.

    - Ingredients of the Preamble
        - The Preamble reveals four ingredients or components:
            - 1. Source of authority of the Constitution: It states that the Constitution derives its authority from the people of India.
            - 2. Nature of the Indian State: It declares India to be a sovereign, socialist, secular, democratic, and republican polity.
            - 3. Objectives of the Constitution: It specifies justice, liberty, equality, and fraternity as the objectives.
            - 4. Date of adoption of the Constitution: It stipulates November 26, 1949, as the date.

    - Key Words in the Preamble
        - The key words—Sovereign, Socialist, Secular, Democratic, Republic, Justice, Liberty, Equality, and Fraternity—are explained as follows:
            - 1. Sovereign
                - The word 'sovereign' implies that India is neither a dependency nor a dominion of any other nation but an independent state, free to conduct its internal and external affairs.
                - Historically, India was a British colony until the Indian Independence Act, 1947. From August 15, 1947, to January 26, 1950, India was a dominion in the British Commonwealth of Nations. On January 26, 1950, India declared herself a sovereign republic, ceasing to be a British dominion. Pakistan, however, remained a dominion until 1956.
                - In 1949, India declared continued full membership of the Commonwealth of Nations, accepting the British Crown as its head. This extra-constitutional declaration does not affect India’s sovereignty. Similarly, India’s membership in the United Nations Organisation (joined 1945) does not limit her sovereignty.
                - To address concerns in the Constituent Assembly, Pandit Nehru stated in 1949: 'We took a pledge long ago to achieve Purna Swaraj. We have achieved it. Does a nation lose its independence by an alliance with another country? Alliance normally means commitments. The free association of the sovereign Commonwealth of Nations does not involve such commitments. Its strength lies in its flexibility and complete freedom. It is open to any member-nation to exit the Commonwealth if it chooses' (Constituent Assembly Debates, 1949). He added, 'It is an agreement by free will, to be terminated by free will'.
                - As a sovereign state, India can acquire foreign territory or cede its territory to a foreign state.
            - 2. Socialist
                - The term 'socialist' was added by the 42nd Amendment Act (1976). Even before this, the Constitution had socialist content through Directive Principles of State Policy, making implicit socialism explicit.
                - The Congress party adopted a resolution in its 1955 Avadi session to establish a 'socialistic pattern of society', stating: 'To realize the objectives of Congress and further the goals in the Preamble and Directive Principles, planning should establish a socialistic pattern where principal means of production are under social ownership or control, production is accelerated, and national wealth is equitably distributed' (Congress Resolution, 1955).
                - Indian socialism is 'democratic socialism', not 'communistic socialism' (state socialism), which involves nationalizing all production and abolishing private property. Democratic socialism supports a mixed economy where public and private sectors coexist. The Supreme Court noted: 'Democratic socialism aims to end poverty, ignorance, disease, and inequality of opportunity' (Nakara vs. Union of India, 1982). It blends Marxism and Gandhism, leaning toward Gandhian socialism (G.B. Pant University vs. State of Uttar Pradesh, 2000).
                - Prime Minister Indira Gandhi clarified: 'We have our own brand of socialism. We will nationalize sectors where necessary. Just nationalization is not our type of socialism'.
                - The 1991 Economic Policy of liberalization, privatization, and globalization has diluted India’s socialist credentials.
            - 3. Secular
                - The term 'secular' was added by the 42nd Amendment Act (1976). The Supreme Court in 1974 noted that, though not explicitly mentioned, the Constitution’s makers intended a secular state, evidenced by Articles 25–28 (guaranteeing freedom of religion).
                - States can be: (a) Atheistic (anti-religion, condemning all religions); (b) Theocratic (pro-religion, with a state religion, e.g., Bangladesh, Pakistan); or (c) Secular (neutral, upholding no particular religion, e.g., USA, India) (C.S. Dande, Constitutional Law of India, 2002, p. 222).
                - Indian secularism embodies a positive concept, granting equal status and support to all religions, irrespective of their strength.
                - Union Law Minister H.R. Gokhale defined it: 'There will be freedom, liberty of faith, and worship, whatever religion you belong to. The State, as a state, will not have any religion except treating every religion equally' (1976). Former Chief Justice P.B. Gajendragadkar added: 'The State does not owe loyalty to any religion; it is not irreligious or anti-religious; it gives equal freedom to all religions'.
            - 4. Democratic
                - The term 'democratic' in the Preamble is derived from Greek words 'Demos' (people) and 'Kratia' (rule), signifying rule by the people based on popular sovereignty.
                - Democracy is direct (people exercise power directly, e.g., Switzerland, via Referendum, Initiative, Recall, Plebiscite) or indirect (representatives exercise power, e.g., parliamentary or presidential systems). Referendum refers proposed legislation to voters; Initiative allows people to propose bills; Recall enables voters to remove representatives; Plebiscite gauges public opinion, often on territorial disputes.
                - India adopts a representative parliamentary democracy, where the executive is responsible to the legislature. Features include universal adult franchise, periodic elections, rule of law, judicial independence, and non-discrimination.
                - 'Democratic' in the Preamble embraces political, social, and economic democracy. Dr. B.R. Ambedkar emphasized: 'Political democracy cannot last unless there lies at its base social democracy, meaning a way of life recognizing liberty, equality, and fraternity as a union of trinity. Divorcing one defeats democracy’s purpose. Without equality, liberty produces supremacy of the few; without liberty, equality kills initiative' (Constituent Assembly, November 25, 1949; B. Shiva Rao, The Framing of India’s Constitution, Vol. IV, p. 944).
                - The Supreme Court (1997) observed: 'The Constitution envisions an egalitarian social order rendering social, economic, and political justice in a social and economic democracy'.
            - 5. Republic
                - A democratic polity can be a monarchy (hereditary head, e.g., Britain) or a republic (elected head, e.g., USA). India’s 'republic' status indicates an elected president, chosen indirectly for five years.
                - It also implies: (a) political sovereignty vests in the people, not a king; (b) no privileged class exists, and all public offices are open to every citizen without discrimination.
            - 6. Justice
                - 'Justice' in the Preamble embraces social, economic, and political forms, secured through Fundamental Rights and Directive Principles.
                - Social justice ensures equal treatment without distinction based on caste, colour, race, religion, or sex, improving conditions for backward classes (SCs, STs, OBCs) and women.
                - Economic justice eliminates inequalities in wealth, income, and property, forming 'distributive justice' with social justice.
                - Political justice grants equal political rights, access to offices, and voice in government.
                - The ideal of justice is inspired by the Russian Revolution (1917).
            - 7. Liberty
                - 'Liberty' means the absence of restraints on individuals while providing opportunities for personal development.
                - The Preamble secures liberty of thought, expression, belief, faith, and worship through Fundamental Rights, enforceable in courts.
                - Liberty is essential for India’s democratic system but is qualified, not absolute, within constitutional limits, not a 'license' to act freely.
                - The ideals of liberty, equality, and fraternity are drawn from the French Revolution (1789–1799).
            - 8. Equality
                - 'Equality' means no special privileges for any section and adequate opportunities for all without discrimination.
                - The Preamble secures equality of status and opportunity, embracing civic, political, and economic dimensions.
                - Civic equality is ensured by Fundamental Rights: (a) Equality before the law (Article 14); (b) Non-discrimination (Article 15); (c) Equal employment opportunity (Article 16); (d) Abolition of untouchability (Article 17); (e) Abolition of titles (Article 18).
                - Political equality is achieved via: (a) No ineligibility for electoral rolls based on religion, race, caste, or sex (Article 325); (b) Adult suffrage for Lok Sabha and state assemblies (Article 326).
                - Economic equality is supported by Directive Principles (Article 39), ensuring equal livelihood rights and pay for men and women.
            - 9. Fraternity
                - 'Fraternity' denotes a sense of brotherhood, promoted by single citizenship and Fundamental Duties (Article 51-A), which mandate harmony and common brotherhood transcending religious, linguistic, regional, or sectional diversities.
                - Fraternity assures: (a) dignity of the individual; (b) unity and integrity of the nation (Integrity added by 42nd Amendment, 1976).
                - K.M. Munshi noted: 'The phrase "dignity of the individual" signifies that the Constitution ensures material betterment, a democratic setup, and recognizes the sacred personality of every individual' (Constituent Assembly Debates).
                - Fundamental Rights, Directive Principles, and Fundamental Duties (e.g., renouncing practices derogatory to women’s dignity, protecting sovereignty) support individual dignity.
                - 'Unity and integrity of the nation' covers psychological and territorial integration. Article 1 describes India as a 'Union of States', implying states cannot secede, ensuring an indestructible Union. It counters communalism, regionalism, casteism, linguism, and secessionism.

    - Significance of the Preamble
        - The Preamble embodies the basic philosophy and fundamental values—political, moral, and religious—on which the Constitution is based. It reflects the Constituent Assembly’s noble vision and the founding fathers’ dreams.
        - Sir Alladi Krishnaswami Iyer stated: 'The Preamble expresses what we had thought or dreamt so long' (Constituent Assembly Debates).
        - K.M. Munshi called it the 'horoscope of our sovereign democratic republic'.
        - Pandit Thakur Das Bhargava described it: 'The Preamble is the most precious part of the Constitution. It is its soul, a key, a jewel, and a yardstick to measure its worth' (Constituent Assembly Debates).
        - Sir Ernest Barker, a distinguished English political scientist, praised it as the 'keynote' to the Constitution, quoting it in his book *Principles of Social and Political Theory* (1951): 'It states in brief and pithy form the argument of much of the book; it may serve as a keynote. I am proud that the people of India begin their independent life by subscribing to principles of a political tradition we in the West call Western, but which is now more than Western'.
        - Former Chief Justice M. Hidayatullah observed: 'The Preamble resembles the Declaration of Independence of the USA but is more than a declaration. It is the soul of our Constitution, laying down the pattern of our political society, containing a solemn resolve only a revolution can alter' (Hidayatullah, *Democracy in India and the Judicial Process*, p. 51).

    - Preamble as Part of the Constitution
        - A controversy existed over whether the Preamble is part of the Constitution.
        - In the Berubari Union case (1960), the Supreme Court held that the Preamble shows the general purposes behind constitutional provisions, serving as a key to the makers’ minds. It aids interpretation when terms are ambiguous but was not considered part of the Constitution (Reference by President under Article 143, 1960, on Indo-Pakistan agreement).
        - In the Kesavananda Bharati case (1973), the Supreme Court reversed this, holding that the Preamble is part of the Constitution, of extreme importance, and should guide its interpretation (Kesavananda Bharati vs. State of Kerala, 1973). This was reaffirmed in the LIC of India case (1995) (LIC of India vs. Consumer Education and Research Centre, 1995).
        - The Preamble was enacted by the Constituent Assembly after the rest of the Constitution to ensure conformity. The Assembly President stated: 'The question is that the Preamble stands part of the Constitution' (Constituent Assembly Debates, 1949), and the motion was adopted, aligning with the Supreme Court’s current view.
        - However:
            - 1. The Preamble is neither a source of legislative power nor a prohibition on such powers.
            - 2. It is non-justiciable, meaning its provisions are not enforceable in courts.

    - Amendability of the Preamble
        - The question of amending the Preamble under Article 368 arose in the Kesavananda Bharati case (1973). Petitioners argued it cannot be amended as it is not part of the Constitution and that amendments cannot destroy basic features enshrined in the Preamble.
        - The Supreme Court held that the Preamble is part of the Constitution, correcting its Berubari Union (1960) opinion. It can be amended under Article 368, provided the basic features are not altered.
        - The Court observed: 'The edifice of our Constitution is based upon the basic elements in the Preamble. Removing any element would collapse the structure, altering its identity. Amending power cannot confer authority to remove these fundamental characteristics' (Kesavananda Bharati, 1973).
        - The Preamble was amended once by the 42nd Constitutional Amendment Act (1976), adding 'Socialist', 'Secular', and 'Integrity'. This was held valid.


- Chapter 6: Union and Its Territory
    - Overview
        - Articles 1 to 4 under Part I of the Constitution deal with the Union and its territory.

    - Union of States
        - Article 1 describes India, that is, Bharat, as a 'Union of States' rather than a 'Federation of States', addressing: (a) the country’s name; (b) type of polity.
        - The Constituent Assembly lacked unanimity on the name. Some preferred the traditional 'Bharat', others the modern 'India', leading to the compromise 'India, that is, Bharat'.
        - The term 'Union' is used despite a federal structure. Dr. B.R. Ambedkar explained: (a) The Indian Federation is not an agreement among states like the American Federation; (b) States have no right to secede. The Union is indestructible, divided into states for administrative convenience (Constituent Assembly Debates).
        - Article 1 classifies India’s territory into three categories:
            - 1. Territories of the states.
            - 2. Union territories.
            - 3. Territories that may be acquired by the Government of India at any time.
        - The First Schedule lists states and union territories with their territorial extent. As of now, there are 28 states and 8 union territories. Constitutional provisions apply uniformly to all states, except special provisions under Part XXI for Maharashtra, Gujarat, Nagaland, Assam, Manipur, Andhra Pradesh, Telangana, Sikkim, Mizoram, Arunachal Pradesh, Goa, and Karnataka, which override general provisions. The Fifth and Sixth Schedules provide for administering scheduled and tribal areas within states.
        - Until 2019, Jammu and Kashmir enjoyed special status under Article 370 with its own constitution. This was abolished by the Constitution (Application to Jammu and Kashmir) Order, 2019, superseding the 1954 order, extending all constitutional provisions to Jammu and Kashmir. The Jammu and Kashmir Reorganisation Act, 2019, bifurcated it into two union territories: Jammu and Kashmir and Ladakh.
        - 'Territory of India' is broader than 'Union of India', including states, union territories, and acquired territories. States share powers with the Centre in the federal system, while union territories and acquired territories are centrally administered.
        - As a sovereign state, India can acquire foreign territories via international law modes: cession (treaty, purchase, gift, lease, plebiscite), occupation (unoccupied land), conquest, or subjugation. Examples include Dadra and Nagar Haveli, Goa, Daman and Diu, Puducherry, and Sikkim.
        - Article 2 empowers Parliament to admit or establish new states on terms it deems fit: (a) admitting existing states; (b) establishing new states. Article 2 applies to states outside the Union, while Article 3 deals with internal reorganization of existing states.

    - Parliament’s Power to Reorganise the States
        - Article 3 authorizes Parliament to:
            - (a) Form a new state by separating territory from a state, uniting states or parts, or adding territory to a state.
            - (b) Increase a state’s area.
            - (c) Diminish a state’s area.
            - (d) Alter state boundaries.
            - (e) Alter state names.
        - Two conditions apply: (a) A bill requires the President’s prior recommendation; (b) The President must refer it to the concerned state legislature for views within a specified period.
        - Parliament is not bound by state legislature views and need not refer amendments to the bill back to the state. For union territories, no reference is needed (Bombay vs. State of Bombay, 1959).
        - The 18th Constitutional Amendment Act (1966) clarified that Parliament can form a new state or union territory by uniting parts of states or union territories.
        - The Constitution allows Parliament to redraw India’s political map without state consent, describing India as 'an indestructible union of destructible states'. States’ territorial integrity is not guaranteed, unlike in the USA, where state consent is required, making it 'an indestructible union of indestructible states'.
        - Article 4 states that laws under Articles 2 and 3 are not constitutional amendments under Article 368, requiring only a simple majority and ordinary legislative process.
        - The Supreme Court (1960) ruled that diminishing a state’s area under Article 3 does not include ceding territory to a foreign country, which requires an Article 368 amendment (Berubari Union case). The 9th Constitutional Amendment Act (1960) transferred Berubari to Pakistan. However, settling boundary disputes (1969 ruling) does not require an amendment, as it involves no cession.
        - Laws under Article 3 are listed in:
            - ------------------------------------------------- Table 6.4 -------------------------------------------------

    - Exchange of Territories with Bangladesh
        - The 100th Constitutional Amendment Act (2015) facilitated India’s acquisition and transfer of territories with Bangladesh per a 1974 agreement and 2011 protocol. India transferred 111 enclaves to Bangladesh, receiving 51 enclaves, alongside adverse possessions and demarcating a 6.1 km border stretch. It modified the First Schedule for Assam, West Bengal, Meghalaya, and Tripura.
        - Background:
            - India and Bangladesh share a 4,096.7 km border, set by the 1947 Radcliffe Award. Disputes led to the 1950 Bagge Award and 1958 Nehru-Noon Agreement. The Berubari Union division required the 9th Amendment (1960), but litigation prevented notification.
            - The 1974 Land Boundary Agreement addressed demarcation, adverse possessions, and enclaves, ratified via the 2011 Protocol with Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura, and West Bengal’s concurrence (Ministry of Law and Justice, Government of India).

    - Evolution of States and Union Territories
        - Integration of Princely States
            - At independence, India comprised British provinces and 552 princely states under native rulers but British paramountcy. The Indian Independence Act (1947) created India and Pakistan, giving princely states three options: join India, join Pakistan, or remain independent. Of 552, 549 joined India; Hyderabad, Junagarh, and Kashmir initially refused but were integrated via police action, referendum, and Instrument of Accession, respectively.
            - In 1950, the Constitution classified states and territories into four categories (29 total): Part A (9 former British provinces), Part B (9 princely states with legislatures), Part C (10 centrally administered states), and Part D (Andaman and Nicobar Islands). See:
                - ------------------------------------------------- Table 6.1 -------------------------------------------------
        - Dhar Commission and JVP Committee
            - Princely state integration was ad hoc, sparking demands, especially in South India, for linguistic reorganization. In June 1948, the Linguistic Provinces Commission, chaired by S.K. Dhar, recommended administrative convenience over language (December 1948 report), causing resentment.
            - In December 1948, Congress formed the JVP Committee (Jawaharlal Nehru, Vallabhbhai Patel, Pattabhi Sitaramayya) without a chairman. Its April 1949 report rejected linguistic reorganization.
            - In October 1953, prolonged agitation and Potti Sriramulu’s 56-day hunger strike death led to creating Andhra, the first linguistic state, from Telugu-speaking areas of Madras.
        - Fazl Ali Commission
            - Andhra’s creation intensified linguistic state demands. In December 1953, the States Reorganisation Commission, chaired by Fazl Ali with K.M. Panikkar and H.N. Kunzru, was formed. Its September 1955 report accepted language as a basis but rejected 'one language-one state', prioritizing India’s unity. Factors considered:
                - (a) Preserving national unity and security.
                - (b) Linguistic and cultural homogeneity.
                - (c) Financial, economic, and administrative considerations.
                - (d) Welfare of state and nation.
            - It proposed abolishing the four-fold classification, creating 16 states and 3 centrally administered territories. The States Reorganisation Act (1956) and 7th Constitutional Amendment Act (1956) implemented this, abolishing Part A, B, and C distinctions, creating 14 states and 6 union territories on November 1, 1956. See:
                - ------------------------------------------------- Table 6.2 -------------------------------------------------
            - Key changes: Kerala formed by merging Travancore-Cochin, Malabar, and Kasaragod; Andhra Pradesh expanded with Hyderabad’s Telugu areas; Madhya Bharat, Vindhya Pradesh, and Bhopal merged into Madhya Pradesh; Saurashtra and Kutch into Bombay; Coorg into Mysore; PEPSU into Punjab; Ajmer into Rajasthan; Laccadive, Minicoy, and Amindivi Islands created from Madras.
        - New States and Union Territories Created After 1956
            - Post-1956, India’s political map changed due to linguistic and cultural demands:
                - Maharashtra and Gujarat (1960): Bombay bifurcated into Maharashtra (Marathi-speaking) and Gujarat (Gujarati-speaking, 15th state) (Bombay Reorganisation Act, 1960).
                - Dadra and Nagar Haveli: Liberated from Portuguese in 1954, became a union territory in 1961 (10th Amendment). Merged with Daman and Diu in 2020 (Dadra and Nagar Haveli and Daman and Diu Merger Act, 2019).
                - Goa, Daman and Diu: Acquired from Portuguese in 1961, became a union territory in 1962 (12th Amendment). Goa gained statehood in 1987 (25th state); Daman and Diu merged with Dadra and Nagar Haveli in 2020 (Goa, Daman and Diu Reorganisation Act, 1987).
                - Puducherry: Former French territories (Puducherry, Karaikal, Mahe, Yanam) handed over in 1954, became a union territory in 1962 (14th Amendment).
                - Nagaland (1963): Formed from Assam’s Naga Hills-Tuensang area (16th state) to address Naga demands (State of Nagaland Act, 1962).
                - Haryana, Chandigarh, and Himachal Pradesh (1966): Punjab bifurcated into Haryana (17th state, Hindi-speaking) and Punjab (Punjabi-speaking), with Chandigarh as a union territory (Punjab Reorganisation Act, 1966). Himachal Pradesh became a state in 1971 (18th state) (State of Himachal Pradesh Act, 1970).
                - Manipur, Tripura, and Meghalaya (1972): Manipur (19th state), Tripura (20th state), and Meghalaya (21st state) gained statehood; Mizoram and Arunachal Pradesh became union territories from Assam (North-Eastern Areas Reorganisation Act, 1971).
                - Sikkim (1975): A princely state until 1947, became India’s protectorate, then an associate state (35th Amendment, 1974). After a 1975 referendum, it became the 22nd state (36th Amendment, 1975).
                - Mizoram, Arunachal Pradesh, and Goa (1987): Mizoram (23rd state), Arunachal Pradesh (24th state), and Goa (25th state) were formed (State of Mizoram Act, State of Arunachal Pradesh Act, Goa, Daman and Diu Reorganisation Act, 1986–1987).
                - Chhattisgarh, Uttarakhand, and Jharkhand (2000): Created from Madhya Pradesh (26th state), Uttar Pradesh (27th state), and Bihar (28th state) (Madhya Pradesh, Uttar Pradesh, Bihar Reorganisation Acts, 2000).
                - Telangana (2014): Carved from Andhra Pradesh (29th state) (Andhra Pradesh Reorganisation Act, 2014).
                - Jammu and Kashmir and Ladakh (2019): Jammu and Kashmir’s special status under Article 370 was abolished (Constitution Order, 2019). It was bifurcated into two union territories (Jammu and Kashmir Reorganisation Act, 2019).
            - Current status (28 states, 8 union territories) is listed in:
                - ------------------------------------------------- Table 6.3 -------------------------------------------------
        - Change of Names
            - State/union territory name changes include:
                - United Provinces to Uttar Pradesh (1950).
                - Madras to Tamil Nadu (1969, Madras State Alteration of Name Act, 1968).
                - Mysore to Karnataka (1973, Mysore State Alteration of Name Act, 1973).
                - Laccadive, Minicoy, and Amindivi Islands to Lakshadweep (1973, Laccadive, Minicoy and Amindivi Islands Alteration of Name Act, 1973).
                - Delhi to National Capital Territory of Delhi (1992, 69th Amendment, 1991).
                - Uttaranchal to Uttarakhand (2006, Uttaranchal Alteration of Name Act, 2006).
                - Pondicherry to Puducherry (2006, Pondicherry Alteration of Name Act, 2006).
                - Orissa to Odisha (2011, Orissa Alteration of Name Act, 2011).
