A forum of Chief Ministers, chaired by one of the Chief Ministers by rotation, can be similarly thought about particularly to coordinate policies of sectors like energy, food, education, environment, and health.
                    - 23. New All-India Services in sectors like health, education, engineering, and judiciary should be created.
                    - 24. Factors inhibiting the composition and functioning of the Second Chamber as a representative forum of states should be removed or modified even if it requires amendment of the Constitutional provisions. In fact, Rajya Sabha offers immense potential to negotiate acceptable solutions to the friction points which emerge between Centre and states in fiscal, legislative, and administrative relations.
                    - 25. A balance of power between states inter se is desirable and this is possible by equality of representation in the Rajya Sabha. This requires amendment of the relevant provisions to give equality of seats to states in the Rajya Sabha, irrespective of their population size.
                    - 26. The scope of devolution of powers to local bodies to act as institutions of self-government should be constitutionally defined through appropriate amendments.
                    - 27. All future Central legislations involving states’ involvement should provide for cost sharing as in the case of the RTE Act. Existing Central legislations where the states are entrusted with the responsibility of implementation should be suitably amended providing for sharing of costs by the Central Government.
                    - 28. The royalty rates on major minerals should be revised at least every three years without any delay. States should be properly compensated for any delay in the revision of royalty beyond three years.
                    - 29. The current ceiling on profession tax should be completely done away with by a Constitutional amendment.
                    - 30. The scope for raising more revenue from the taxes mentioned in Article 268 should be examined afresh. This issue may be either referred to the next Finance Commission or an expert committee be appointed to look into the matter.
                    - 31. To bring greater accountability, all fiscal legislations should provide for an annual assessment by an independent body and the reports of these bodies should be laid in both Houses of Parliament/state legislature.
                    - 32. Considerations specified in the Terms of Reference (ToR) of the Finance Commission should be even-handed as between the Centre and the states. There should be an effective mechanism to involve the states in the finalisation of the ToR of the Finance Commissions.
                    - 33. The Central Government should review all the existing cesses and surcharges with a view to bringing down their share in the gross tax revenue.
                    - 34. Because of the close linkages between the plan and non-plan expenditure, an expert committee may be appointed to look into the issue of distinction between the plan and non-plan expenditure.
                    - 35. There should be much better coordination between the Finance Commission and the Planning Commission. The synchronisation of the periods covered by the Finance Commission and the Five-Year Plan will considerably improve such coordination.
                    - 36. The Finance Commission division in the Ministry of Finance should be converted into a full-fledged department, serving as the permanent secretariat for the Finance Commissions.
                    - 37. The Planning Commission has a crucial role in the current situation. But its role should be that of coordination rather than of micro-managing sectoral plans of the Central ministries and the states.
                    - 38. Steps should be taken for the setting up of an Inter-State Trade and Commerce Commission under Article 307 read with Entry 42 of List I. This Commission should be vested with both advisory and executive roles with decision-making powers. As a Constitutional body, the decisions of the Commission should be final and binding on all states as well as the Union of India. Any party aggrieved with the decision of the Commission may prefer an appeal to the Supreme Court.
                - The recommendations contained in the Report of the Punchhi Commission have been considered by the Standing Committee of the Inter-State Council in its meetings held in 2017 and 2018. The recommendations of the Commission along with the recommendations of the Standing Committee thereon, have been circulated to the state governments for comments.
                    - Annual Report 2021–22, Ministry of Home Affairs, Government of India, p. 246.

- Chapter 16: Inter-State Relations
    - Introduction
        - The effective functioning of India’s federal system relies on harmonious relations and close cooperation not only between the Centre and the states but also among the states themselves. To facilitate inter-state comity, the Constitution includes provisions for:
            - 1. Adjudication of inter-state water disputes.
            - 2. Coordination through inter-state councils.
            - 3. Mutual recognition of public acts, records, and judicial proceedings.
            - 4. Freedom of inter-state trade, commerce, and intercourse.
        - Additionally, Parliament has established zonal councils under the States Reorganisation Act, 1956, to promote inter-state cooperation and coordination.

    - Inter-State Water Disputes
        - Article 262 provides for the adjudication of inter-state water disputes, with two key provisions:
            - (i) Parliament may by law provide for the adjudication of disputes or complaints regarding the use, distribution, and control of waters of any inter-state river or river valley.
            - (ii) Parliament may bar the Supreme Court or any other court from exercising jurisdiction over such disputes or complaints.
        - Pursuant to Article 262, Parliament has enacted two laws:
            - The **River Boards Act, 1956**, which allows the Central government to establish river boards, upon the request of concerned state governments, to regulate and develop inter-state rivers and river valleys.
            - The **Inter-State River Water Disputes Act, 1956**, which empowers the Central government to constitute ad hoc tribunals to adjudicate disputes between two or more states concerning inter-state river or river valley waters. Tribunal decisions are final and binding, and neither the Supreme Court nor any other court has jurisdiction over disputes referred to such tribunals.
        - The Central government has established several inter-state water dispute tribunals, detailed in:
            - ------------------------------------------------- Table 16.1 -------------------------------------------------

    - Inter-State Councils
        - Article 263 provides for the establishment of an Inter-State Council to promote coordination between states and between the Centre and states. The President may establish such a council if it serves the public interest, defining its duties, organization, and procedure.
        - Although the President can specify the council’s duties, Article 263 outlines three possible functions:
            - (a) Enquiring into and advising on disputes between states.
            - (b) Investigating and discussing subjects of common interest to the states or the Centre and states.
            - (c) Making recommendations on such subjects, particularly for better coordination of policy and action.
        - The Inter-State Council’s role in addressing inter-state disputes complements the Supreme Court’s jurisdiction under Article 131 to resolve legal controversies between governments. The Council handles both legal and non-legal disputes but provides advisory recommendations, unlike the Court’s binding decisions (M.P. Jain, *Indian Constitutional Law*, Wadhwa, Fourth Edition, p. 382).
        - Under Article 263, the President has established several councils to enhance policy coordination in specific areas:
            - Central Council of Health and Family Welfare.
            - Central Council of Local Government (originally Central Council of Local Self-Government, established in 1954).
            - Four Regional Councils for Sales Tax for Northern, Eastern, Western, and Southern Zones.
            - Note: The Central Council of Indian Medicine and Central Council of Homoeopathy were established under separate Acts of Parliament.
        - Establishment of Inter-State Council
            - The Sarkaria Commission on Centre-State Relations (1983–88) strongly advocated for a permanent Inter-State Council under Article 263, recommending it be called the **Inter-Governmental Council** to distinguish it from other bodies under the same article. The Commission suggested the Council focus on duties under clauses (b) and (c) of Article 263.
            - Following these recommendations, the Janata Dal government, led by V.P. Singh, established the Inter-State Council on May 28, 1990, via the Inter-State Council Order. The Council comprises:
                - (i) Prime Minister as Chairman.
                - (ii) Chief Ministers of all states.
                - (iii) Chief Ministers of union territories with legislative assemblies.
                - (iv) Administrators of union territories without legislative assemblies.
                - (v) Governors of states under President’s rule.
                - (vi) Six Central Cabinet Ministers, including the Home Minister, nominated by the Prime Minister.
            - Five Ministers of Cabinet rank or Minister of State (independent charge), nominated by the Prime Minister, are permanent invitees.
            - The Council is a recommendatory body, focusing on:
                - Investigating and discussing subjects of common interest.
                - Making recommendations for better policy and action coordination.
                - Deliberating on matters of general interest referred by the Chairman.
            - The Council meets at least three times annually, with meetings held in camera and decisions made by consensus.
            - A Standing Committee, established in 1996, facilitates continuous consultation and processes matters for the Council’s consideration. It includes:
                - (i) Union Home Minister as Chairman.
                - (ii) Five Union Cabinet Ministers.
                - (iii) Nine Chief Ministers.
            - The Council is supported by the Inter-State Council Secretariat, established in 1991 and headed by a Secretary to the Government of India. Since 2011, it also serves as the secretariat for the Zonal Councils.

    - Public Acts, Records, and Judicial Proceedings
        - As each state’s jurisdiction is limited to its territory, acts and records of one state may not be recognized in another. To address this, the Constitution’s “Full Faith and Credit” clause (Article 261) provides:
            - (i) Full faith and credit must be given throughout India to public acts, records, and judicial proceedings of the Centre and every state. Public acts include legislative and executive actions, while public records encompass official books, registers, or records made by public servants in their official duties.
            - (ii) Parliament may prescribe the manner and conditions for proving such acts, records, and proceedings and their effect, allowing it to regulate their recognition across states.
            - (iii) Final judgments and orders of civil courts anywhere in India are executable nationwide without requiring a fresh suit. This applies only to civil judgments, not criminal ones, meaning courts are not required to enforce another state’s penal laws.

    - Inter-State Trade and Commerce
        - Articles 301 to 307 in Part XIII of the Constitution govern trade, commerce, and intercourse within India.
        - Article 301 ensures that trade, commerce, and intercourse throughout India shall be free, aiming to eliminate border barriers between states and foster a unified economic unit. This freedom applies to both inter-state and intra-state trade, commerce, and intercourse, with violations occurring if restrictions are imposed at state borders or any other stage.
        - The freedom under Article 301 is subject to restrictions outlined in Articles 302 to 305:
            - (i) Parliament may impose restrictions on trade, commerce, and intercourse between or within states in the public interest. However, it cannot favor one state over another or discriminate between states, except in cases of scarcity of goods (e.g., the Essential Commodities Act, 1955, allows the Central government to control production, supply, and distribution of commodities like petroleum, coal, iron, and steel).
            - (ii) State legislatures may impose reasonable restrictions on trade, commerce, and intercourse within or with their state in the public interest, but only with the President’s prior sanction. States cannot favor one state over another or discriminate between them.
            - (iii) States may impose taxes on goods imported from other states or union territories equivalent to taxes on similar locally manufactured goods, preventing discriminatory taxation.
            - (iv) The freedom under Article 301 is subject to nationalization laws, allowing Parliament or state legislatures to create monopolies for the Centre or states, fully or partially excluding citizens or others.
        - Parliament may appoint an authority to implement these provisions and confer necessary powers, but no such authority has been established (unlike the Interstate Commerce Commission in the USA).
    
    - Zonal Councils
        - Zonal Councils are statutory bodies established under the States Reorganisation Act, 1956, not constitutional bodies. The Act divided India into five zones—Northern, Central, Eastern, Western, and Southern—each with a zonal council.
        - Zones were formed considering natural divisions, river systems, means of communication, cultural and linguistic affinities, and requirements for economic development, security, and law and order.
        - Each Zonal Council comprises:
            - (a) Union Home Minister.
            - (b) Chief Ministers of all states in the zone.
            - (c) Two other ministers from each state.
            - (d) Administrators of union territories in the zone.
        - Advisors (without voting rights) include:
            - (i) A nominee from NITI Aayog.
            - (ii) Chief Secretary of each state government in the zone.
            - (iii) Development Commissioner of each state in the zone.
        - The Union Home Minister serves as Chairman of all five councils, with Chief Ministers acting as Vice-Chairmen on a rotational basis, holding office for one year.
        - Zonal Councils promote cooperation and coordination among states, union territories, and the Centre, discussing and recommending on issues like economic and social planning, linguistic minorities, border disputes, and inter-state transport. They are deliberative and advisory bodies.
        - Their objectives include:
            - Achieving emotional integration of the country.
            - Curbing acute state-consciousness, regionalism, linguism, and particularistic trends.
            - Mitigating after-effects of reorganization to synchronize integration and economic advancement.
            - Facilitating Centre-state cooperation in social and economic matters, exchanging ideas, and evolving uniform policies.
            - Ensuring speedy execution of major development projects.
            - Securing political equilibrium across regions.
        - North-Eastern Council
            - Established under the North-Eastern Council Act, 1971, it includes Assam, Manipur, Mizoram, Arunachal Pradesh, Nagaland, Meghalaya, Tripura, and Sikkim (added in 2002, effective August 8, 1972).
            - Its functions resemble those of Zonal Councils but include formulating a unified regional plan for common matters and reviewing measures for security and public order in the region.
        - Zonal Councils’ composition and headquarters are detailed in:
            - ------------------------------------------------- Table 16.2 -------------------------------------------------
        - Articles related to inter-state relations are summarized in:
            - ------------------------------------------------- Table 16.3 -------------------------------------------------


- Chapter 17: Emergency Provisions
    - Introduction
        - The Emergency provisions, contained in Part XVIII of the Constitution (Articles 352 to 360), enable the Central government to address abnormal situations effectively. Their inclusion aims to safeguard India’s sovereignty, unity, integrity, security, democratic political system, and Constitution.
        - During an Emergency, the Central government becomes all-powerful, and states come under its total control, transforming the federal structure into a unitary one without formal constitutional amendment. This shift from federalism in normal times to unitarism during emergencies is a unique feature of the Indian Constitution. Dr. B.R. Ambedkar observed in the Constituent Assembly:
            - “All federal systems, including the American, are placed in a tight mould of federalism. No matter what the circumstances, it cannot change its form and shape. It can never be unitary. On the other hand, the Constitution of India can be both unitary as well as federal according to the requirements of time and circumstances. In normal times, it is framed to work as a federal system. But in times of Emergency, it is so designed as to make it work as though it was a unitary system.”
        - The Constitution provides for three types of emergencies:
            - 1. National Emergency due to war, external aggression, or armed rebellion (Article 352).
            - 2. President’s Rule due to the failure of constitutional machinery in states (Article 356).
            - 3. Financial Emergency due to a threat to India’s financial stability or credit (Article 360).

    - National Emergency
        - Grounds of Declaration
            - Under Article 352, the President can declare a National Emergency if the security of India or any part thereof is threatened by war, external aggression, or armed rebellion. The President may act even before the actual occurrence if satisfied of imminent danger.
            - The President can issue different proclamations for war, external aggression, armed rebellion, or imminent danger thereof, whether or not another proclamation is in operation (added by the 38th Amendment Act, 1975).
            - A National Emergency declared due to war or external aggression is termed “External Emergency,” while one due to armed rebellion is called “Internal Emergency.”
            - The proclamation may apply to the entire country or a specific part, as enabled by the 42nd Amendment Act, 1976.
            - Originally, “internal disturbance” was a ground for declaration, but the 44th Amendment Act, 1978, replaced it with “armed rebellion” to narrow its scope, preventing declarations like the 1975 Emergency under vague grounds.
            - The President can declare a National Emergency only after receiving a written recommendation from the Cabinet, not merely on the Prime Minister’s advice (44th Amendment Act, 1978). This safeguard was introduced after the 1975 Emergency, when Prime Minister Indira Gandhi advised the President without consulting the Cabinet, informing it post-facto as a fait accompli.
            - The 38th Amendment Act, 1975, made National Emergency declarations immune from judicial review, but this was repealed by the 44th Amendment Act, 1978. In the Minerva Mills case (1980), the Supreme Court held that proclamations can be challenged if based on mala fide, extraneous, irrelevant, absurd, or perverse grounds.
        - Parliamentary Approval and Duration
            - The proclamation must be approved by both Houses of Parliament within one month of issuance, reduced from two months by the 44th Amendment Act, 1978. If the Lok Sabha is dissolved or dissolves during this period, the proclamation survives until 30 days from the first sitting of the reconstituted Lok Sabha, provided the Rajya Sabha approves it.
            - Once approved, the Emergency continues for six months and can be extended indefinitely with parliamentary approval every six months (44th Amendment Act, 1978). Previously, it could remain in force as long as the Executive desired.
            - If the Lok Sabha dissolves during the six-month period without approving continuance, the proclamation survives until 30 days from the reconstituted Lok Sabha’s first sitting, provided the Rajya Sabha approves it.
            - Resolutions approving the proclamation or its continuance require a special majority: (a) a majority of the total membership of each House, and (b) a majority of not less than two-thirds of members present and voting (44th Amendment Act, 1978). Previously, a simple majority sufficed.
        - Revocation of Proclamation
            - The President may revoke a proclamation at any time by a subsequent proclamation, which does not require parliamentary approval.
            - The President must revoke the proclamation if the Lok Sabha passes a resolution disapproving its continuance (44th Amendment Act, 1978). Previously, revocation was at the President’s discretion without Lok Sabha control.
            - If one-tenth of the Lok Sabha’s total members give written notice to the Speaker (or President, if the House is not in session), a special sitting must be held within 14 days to consider a disapproval resolution (44th Amendment Act, 1978).
            - A disapproval resolution differs from an approval resolution:
                - 1. Disapproval requires only Lok Sabha passage, while approval needs both Houses.
                - 2. Disapproval needs a simple majority, while approval requires a special majority.
        - Effects of National Emergency
            - A National Emergency has significant effects on the political system, categorized into three areas:
                - 1. Centre-state relations.
                - 2. Life of the Lok Sabha and state assemblies.
                - 3. Fundamental Rights.
            - Effect on Centre-State Relations
                - (a) Executive: The Centre’s executive power extends to directing states on how to exercise their executive powers, covering all matters (not just specified ones as in normal times). States remain operational but under complete Central control.
                - (b) Legislative: Parliament can legislate on State List subjects, overriding state legislatures’ powers, though the latter are not suspended. The Constitution becomes unitary. Laws made by Parliament on state subjects cease six months after the Emergency ends. The President can issue ordinances on state subjects if Parliament is not in session, and Parliament can confer powers or impose duties on the Centre for matters outside the Union List (42nd Amendment Act, 1976, extended these effects to all states).
                - (c) Financial: The President can modify revenue distribution between the Centre and states, reducing or cancelling transfers until the financial year in which the Emergency ends. Such orders must be laid before both Houses of Parliament.
            - Effect on the Life of the Lok Sabha and State Assembly
                - The Lok Sabha’s term (five years) can be extended by Parliament for one year at a time during a National Emergency, up to six months after the Emergency ends. For example, the Fifth Lok Sabha (1971–1977) was extended twice by one year (to March 1977 and March 1978) but dissolved on January 18, 1977.
                - State legislative assemblies’ terms can similarly be extended by one year at a time, up to six months post-Emergency.
            - Effect on Fundamental Rights
                - (a) Suspension of Fundamental Rights under Article 19: Under Article 358, the six Fundamental Rights under Article 19 are automatically suspended during a National Emergency declared due to war or external aggression (not armed rebellion, per the 44th Amendment Act, 1978). The state can make laws or take executive actions inconsistent with Article 19, which cannot be challenged. These rights revive post-Emergency, but actions during the Emergency remain immune. Only laws related to the Emergency are protected (44th Amendment Act, 1978).
                - (b) Suspension of Other Fundamental Rights: Under Article 359, the President can suspend the right to move courts for enforcing specified Fundamental Rights (except Articles 20 and 21, per the 44th Amendment Act, 1978). The rights themselves remain, but enforcement is suspended for the period specified in the Presidential Order, which must be approved by Parliament. Laws or actions inconsistent with specified rights cannot be challenged during the Order’s operation, but only Emergency-related laws are protected.
        - Distinction Between Articles 358 and 359
            - 1. Article 358 applies only to Article 19, while Article 359 covers all Fundamental Rights specified in the Presidential Order.
            - 2. Article 358 automatically suspends Article 19 upon Emergency declaration, while Article 359 requires a Presidential Order to suspend enforcement.
            - 3. Article 358 applies only to External Emergency (war or external aggression), while Article 359 applies to both External and Internal Emergencies.
            - 4. Article 358 suspends Article 19 for the entire Emergency duration, while Article 359 suspends enforcement for a period specified by the President.
            - 5. Article 358 applies nationwide, while Article 359 may apply to the entire country or a part.
            - 6. Article 358 fully suspends Article 19, while Article 359 cannot suspend enforcement of Articles 20 and 21.
            - 7. Both provide immunity only for Emergency-related laws and actions.
        - Declarations Made So Far
            - National Emergency has been proclaimed three times:
                - 1962 (October) due to Chinese aggression in NEFA (now Arunachal Pradesh), revoked in January 1968.
                - 1971 (December) due to the India-Pakistan war, revoked in March 1977.
                - 1975 (June) due to internal disturbance, revoked in March 1977.
            - The 1975 Emergency was controversial due to alleged misuse. The Janata Party government (1977) appointed the Shah Commission, which found the declaration unjustified. The 44th Amendment Act, 1978, introduced safeguards to prevent such misuse.

    - President’s Rule
        - Grounds of Imposition
            - Article 355 obliges the Centre to protect states against external aggression and internal disturbance and ensure governance per the Constitution. Article 356 allows the President to impose President’s Rule if satisfied that a state’s government cannot function constitutionally, based on the governor’s report or otherwise. Article 365 permits imposition if a state fails to comply with Central directions.
        - Parliamentary Approval and Duration
            - A proclamation requires approval by both Houses of Parliament within two months. If the Lok Sabha is dissolved, the proclamation survives until 30 days from the reconstituted Lok Sabha’s first sitting, provided the Rajya Sabha approves it.
            - Once approved, President’s Rule lasts six months, extendable up to three years with parliamentary approval every six months (44th Amendment Act, 1978, reduced from one year per the 42nd Amendment Act, 1976). The 68th Amendment Act, 1991, allowed a five-year extension in Punjab (1987).
            - Beyond one year, extensions require: (a) a National Emergency in India or the state, and (b) Election Commission certification that elections cannot be held.
            - Approvals require a simple majority. Proclamations can be revoked by the President without parliamentary approval.
        - Consequences of President’s Rule
            - The President gains extraordinary powers:
                - 1. Assumes functions of the state government and powers of the governor or other executive authorities.
                - 2. Declares that state legislature powers are exercised by Parliament.
                - 3. Takes necessary steps, including suspending constitutional provisions related to state bodies.
            - The state council of ministers is dismissed, and the governor administers the state with the chief secretary or advisors. The state legislature is suspended or dissolved, and Parliament passes state bills and budgets. If dissolved, fresh elections are held.
            - Parliament can delegate law-making to the President or another authority. The President can authorize expenditure from the state consolidated fund when the Lok Sabha is not in session, pending parliamentary sanction.
            - Laws made by Parliament or the President remain operative post-President’s Rule but can be repealed by the state legislature. The state high court’s powers remain unaffected.
            - A comparison of National Emergency and President’s Rule is provided in:
                - ------------------------------------------------- Table 17.1 -------------------------------------------------
        - Use of Article 356
            - Since 1950, President’s Rule has been imposed over 125 times, often arbitrarily for political reasons, making Article 356 highly controversial. It was first imposed in Punjab in 1951 and has affected nearly all states.
            - In 1977, the Janata Party imposed President’s Rule in nine Congress-ruled states (Rajasthan, Uttar Pradesh, Madhya Pradesh, Punjab, Bihar, Himachal Pradesh, Odisha, West Bengal, Haryana) citing loss of electoral mandate. In 1980, Congress retaliated similarly in nine states (Uttar Pradesh, Bihar, Rajasthan, Madhya Pradesh, Punjab, Odisha, Gujarat, Maharashtra, Tamil Nadu).
            - In 1992, President’s Rule was imposed in three BJP-ruled states (Madhya Pradesh, Himachal Pradesh, Rajasthan) for not enforcing a Central ban on religious organizations. The Bommai case (1994) upheld this but struck down impositions in Nagaland (1988), Karnataka (1989), and Meghalaya (1991).
            - Dr. B.R. Ambedkar hoped Article 356 would remain a “dead-letter,” used only as a last resort, but it has been a “deadly-weapon” against state governments.
        - Scope of Judicial Review
            - The 38th Amendment Act, 1975, made the President’s satisfaction under Article 356 immune from judicial review, but the 44th Amendment Act, 1978, restored reviewability. The Bommai case (1994) established:
                - 1. Proclamations are subject to judicial review.
                - 2. The President’s satisfaction must be based on relevant material, not mala fide or extraneous grounds.
                - 3. The Centre must prove the material’s relevance.
                - 4. Courts can assess material relevance, not correctness.
                - 5. Unconstitutional proclamations allow restoration of the state government and legislature.
                - 6. Dissolution requires parliamentary approval; otherwise, the legislature is suspended.
                - 7. Anti-secular governance justifies action under Article 356.
                - 8. Confidence issues must be tested on the assembly floor.
                - 9. New Central governments cannot dismiss state ministries of other parties.
                - 10. Article 356 is an exceptional power for special situations.
        - Cases of Proper and Improper Use
            - Per the Sarkaria Commission (1988) and Bommai case (1994), proper uses include:
                - 1. Hung assembly with no majority party.
                - 2. Majority party declines to form a ministry, and no coalition is possible.
                - 3. Ministry resigns after defeat, and no alternative is feasible.
                - 4. State disregards Central constitutional directions.
                - 5. Internal subversion against the Constitution or law.
                - 6. Physical breakdown endangering state security.
            - Improper uses include:
                - 1. Dismissing a ministry without exploring alternatives.
                - 2. Governor assessing ministry support without a floor test.
                - 3. Imposition after Lok Sabha election defeats (e.g., 1977, 1980).
                - 4. Internal disturbances not amounting to subversion.
                - 5. Maladministration, corruption, or financial exigencies.
                - 6. No prior warning to the state, except in extreme urgency.
                - 7. Addressing intra-party issues or extraneous purposes.

    - Financial Emergency
        - Grounds of Declaration
            - Article 360 allows the President to proclaim a Financial Emergency if satisfied that India’s financial stability or credit (or any part thereof) is threatened. The 38th Amendment Act, 1975, made the President’s satisfaction final, but the 44th Amendment Act, 1978, restored judicial review.
        - Parliamentary Approval and Duration
            - The proclamation requires approval by both Houses within two months. If the Lok Sabha is dissolved, it survives until 30 days from the reconstituted Lok Sabha’s first sitting, provided the Rajya Sabha approves it.
            - Once approved, it continues indefinitely until revoked, with no maximum period or need for repeated approvals. A simple majority suffices for approval.
            - The President can revoke it without parliamentary approval.
        - Effects of Financial Emergency
            - 1. The Centre can direct states to observe financial propriety or issue other necessary directions.
            - 2. Directions may include reducing salaries or allowances of state employees or reserving money/financial bills for the President’s consideration after state legislature passage.
            - 3. The President can reduce salaries or allowances of Union employees, including Supreme Court and high court judges.
            - The Centre gains full financial control over states. H.N. Kunzru noted this threatens state financial autonomy. Dr. B.R. Ambedkar justified it by referencing the U.S. National Recovery Act (1933) for economic crisis management. No Financial Emergency has been declared, despite the 1991 financial crisis.
    
    - Criticism of the Emergency Provisions
        - Constituent Assembly critics argued:
            - 1. The federal character is destroyed, empowering the Union excessively.
            - 2. Union executive centralizes all powers.
            - 3. The President becomes a dictator.
            - 4. State financial autonomy is nullified.
            - 5. Fundamental Rights become meaningless, undermining democracy.
        - H.V. Kamath feared a totalitarian state, K.T. Shah called it reactionary, T.T. Krishnamachari warned of constitutional dictatorship, and H.N. Kunzru highlighted threats to state autonomy.
        - Supporters like Alladi Krishnaswami Ayyar called the provisions the “life-breath” of the Constitution, and Mahabir Tyagi viewed them as a “safety-valve.” Dr. B.R. Ambedkar acknowledged potential misuse but defended their necessity.
        - Articles related to Emergency provisions are summarized in:
            - ------------------------------------------------- Table 17.2 -------------------------------------------------


- CHAPTER 18: PRESIDENT
    - Introduction
        - Articles 52 to 78 in Part V of the Constitution deal with the Union executive, which consists of:
            - 1. The President.
            - 2. The Vice-President.
            - 3. The Prime Minister.
            - 4. The Council of Ministers.
            - 5. The Attorney General of India.
        - The President is the head of the Indian State, the first citizen of India, and acts as the symbol of unity, integrity, and solidarity of the nation.

    - Election of the President
        - The President is elected indirectly by an electoral college consisting of:
            - 1. The elected members of both Houses of Parliament.
            - 2. The elected members of the legislative assemblies of the states.
            - 3. The elected members of the legislative assemblies of the Union Territories of Delhi and Puducherry.
                - This provision was added by the 70th Constitutional Amendment Act of 1992, effective from June 1, 1995.
        - Nominated members of both Houses of Parliament, nominated members of state legislative assemblies, members (elected or nominated) of state legislative councils (in bicameral legislatures), and nominated members of the legislative assemblies of Delhi and Puducherry do not participate in the presidential election.
        - The 104th Constitutional Amendment Act of 2019 discontinued the nomination of Anglo-Indian members to the Lok Sabha and state legislative assemblies, effective from January 25, 2020.
        - If a legislative assembly is dissolved, its members cease to be qualified to vote in the presidential election, even if fresh elections are not held before the election.
        - To ensure uniformity in representation and parity between states and the Union, the number of votes for each elected member is determined as follows:
            - 1. For a state legislative assembly member:
                - Value of the vote of an MLA = (Total population of state / Total number of elected members in the state legislative assembly) x (1/1000)
                - Per the 84th Constitutional Amendment Act of 2001, 'population' refers to the 1971 census until figures from the first census after 2026 are published.
            - 2. For a member of Parliament:
                - Value of the vote of an MP = Total value of votes of all MLAs of all states / Total number of elected members of Parliament
        - The values of votes for MLAs, states, MPs, and the electoral college for the 2022 presidential election are detailed in:
            - ------------------------------------------------- Table 18.4 -------------------------------------------------
        - The election follows the system of proportional representation by single transferable vote, with voting by secret ballot. A candidate must secure an absolute majority, determined by:
            - Electoral quota = (Total number of valid votes polled / 2) + 1
        - Voters mark preferences (1, 2, 3, etc.) on a single ballot. First preference votes are counted; if no candidate secures the quota, the candidate with the least votes is eliminated, and their second preferences are transferred. This continues until a candidate achieves the quota.
        - Disputes regarding the election are resolved by the Supreme Court, whose decision is final. The election cannot be challenged due to an incomplete electoral college. If an election is declared void, acts performed by the President before the declaration remain valid.
        - The Constituent Assembly opted for indirect election to align with the parliamentary system, where the President is a nominal executive, and to avoid the cost and complexity of direct elections. Election by Parliament alone was rejected to ensure the President represents both the Union and states equally.
        - The term 'proportional representation' is a misnomer, as only one vacancy exists; 'preferential vote' is more accurate. Similarly, 'single transferable vote' is debated, as voters have plural preferences.
        - Presidential elections from 1952 to 2022 are summarized in:
            - ------------------------------------------------- Table 18.1 -------------------------------------------------

    - Qualifications, Oath, and Conditions
        - Qualifications for Election as President
            - A candidate must:
                - 1. Be a citizen of India.
                - 2. Have completed 35 years of age.
                - 3. Be qualified for election as a member of the Lok Sabha.
                - 4. Not hold any office of profit under the Union, state, local, or public authority.
                    - A sitting President, Vice-President, Governor, or Union/state minister is not deemed to hold an office of profit.
                    - Per the Presidential and Vice-Presidential Elections Act of 1952, amended in 1997.
            - Nominations require at least 50 electors as proposers and 50 as seconders. A candidate must deposit Rs. 15,000 with the Reserve Bank of India, forfeitable if they fail to secure one-sixth of the votes. Before 1997, the requirement was 10 proposers and seconders and a Rs. 2,500 deposit.
        - Oath or Affirmation by the President
            - Before assuming office, the President swears to:
                - 1. Faithfully execute the office.
                - 2. Preserve, protect, and defend the Constitution and the law.
                - 3. Devote themselves to the service and well-being of the people of India.
            - The oath is administered by the Chief Justice of India or, in their absence, the seniormost Supreme Court judge available. Acting Presidents also take this oath.
        - Conditions of President's Office
            - The President:
                - 1. Must not be a member of Parliament or a state legislature; if elected, they vacate such a seat upon assuming office.
                - 2. Must not hold any other office of profit.
                - 3. Is entitled to rent-free residence at Rashtrapati Bhavan.
                - 4. Receives emoluments, allowances, and privileges as determined by Parliament.
                - 5. Has emoluments and allowances that cannot be reduced during their term.
            - In 2018, the President’s salary increased from Rs. 1.5 lakh to Rs. 5 lakh per month, effective January 1, 2016, per the Finance Act, 2018, amending the President’s Emoluments and Pension Act, 1951.
            - In 2008, the pension for retired Presidents was set at 50% of their salary, with additional benefits including a furnished residence, phone, car, medical treatment, travel, secretarial staff, and office expenses up to Rs. 1 lakh annually. Spouses of deceased Presidents receive a pension at 50% of the retired President’s pension, with similar benefits up to Rs. 20,000 annually in office expenses, per the President’s Emoluments and Pension Amendment Act of 2008.
            - The President enjoys immunity from legal liability for official acts, criminal proceedings during their term (even for personal acts), and arrest or imprisonment. Civil proceedings for personal acts may be initiated after two months’ notice.

    - Term, Impeachment, and Vacancy
        - Term of President's Office
            - The President serves a five-year term from the date of assuming office. They may resign by addressing a letter to the Vice-President or be removed via impeachment. They can hold office beyond their term until a successor assumes charge and are eligible for re-election without a term limit. Only Dr. Rajendra Prasad served two terms.
        - Impeachment of President
            - The President may be impeached for 'violation of the Constitution,' though the term is undefined. Charges can be initiated in either House of Parliament, signed by one-fourth of its members, with 14 days’ notice to the President. The resolution requires a two-thirds majority of the House’s total membership. The other House investigates, with the President entitled to appear or be represented. If the second House passes the resolution by a two-thirds majority, the President is removed from the date of passage.
            - Nominated members of Parliament participate in impeachment, unlike the election, while state and Union Territory assembly members do not. No President has been impeached.
        - Vacancy in the President's Office
            - A vacancy arises due to:
                - 1. Expiry of the five-year term.
                - 2. Resignation.
                - 3. Impeachment.
                - 4. Death (e.g., Dr. Zakir Hussain and Fakhruddin Ali Ahmed died in office).
                - 5. Disqualification or void election.
            - Elections to fill a vacancy due to term expiry must occur before the term ends. If delayed, the outgoing President continues until a successor assumes office. For vacancies due to resignation, removal, death, or otherwise, an election must be held within six months, and the new President serves a full five-year term.
            - The Vice-President acts as President during such vacancies or when the President is unable to discharge duties due to absence, illness, or other causes. If the Vice-President’s office is vacant, the Chief Justice of India or the seniormost Supreme Court judge acts as President. For example, after Dr. Zakir Hussain’s death in 1969, Vice-President V.V. Giri acted as President, followed by Chief Justice M. Hidayatullah from July 20 to August 24, 1969.
            - Acting Presidents enjoy the same powers, immunities, and emoluments as the President.

    - Powers and Functions of the President
        - The President’s powers and functions are categorized as:
            - 1. Executive powers.
            - 2. Legislative powers.
            - 3. Financial powers.
            - 4. Judicial powers.
            - 5. Diplomatic powers.
            - 6. Military powers.
            - 7. Emergency powers.
        - Executive Powers
            - The President:
                - (a) Takes all executive actions of the Union government in their name.
                - (b) Makes rules specifying how orders and instruments made in their name are authenticated.
                - (c) Makes rules for the convenient transaction of Union government business and its allocation among ministers.
                - (d) Appoints the Prime Minister and other ministers, who hold office during their pleasure.
                - (e) Appoints the Attorney General of India and determines their remuneration, holding office during their pleasure.
                - (f) Appoints the Comptroller and Auditor General, Chief Election Commissioner, other Election Commissioners, Chairman and members of the Union Public Service Commission, Governors, and Finance Commission members.
                - (g) May seek information from the Prime Minister on Union administration and legislative proposals.
                - (h) May require the Prime Minister to submit for Council of Ministers’ consideration any matter decided by a minister but not considered by the Council.
                - (i) May appoint a commission to investigate conditions of backward classes.
                - (j) May appoint an inter-state council to promote Centre-state and inter-state cooperation.
                - (k) Directly administers union territories through appointed administrators.
                - (l) May declare areas as scheduled areas and has powers over their administration and tribal areas.
        - Legislative Powers
            - The President, an integral part of Parliament, has the following powers:
                - (a) Summons or prorogues Parliament and dissolves the Lok Sabha; may summon a joint sitting of both Houses, presided over by the Lok Sabha Speaker.
                - (b) Addresses Parliament at the first session after each general election and each year’s first session.
                - (c) Sends messages to Parliament regarding pending bills or other matters.
                - (d) Appoints a Lok Sabha member to preside when both Speaker and Deputy Speaker offices are vacant, and similarly for the Rajya Sabha when Chairman and Deputy Chairman offices are vacant.
                - (e) Nominates 12 Rajya Sabha members with special knowledge or experience in literature, science, art, or social service.
                - (f) Nominated two Lok Sabha members from the Anglo-Indian community until 2020, discontinued by the 104th Constitutional Amendment Act of 2019.
                - (g) Decides on member disqualifications in consultation with the Election Commission.
                - (h) Requires prior recommendation or permission for bills involving expenditure from the Consolidated Fund, state boundary alterations, money bills, tax variations affecting states, redefining 'agricultural income,' or affecting state revenue distribution.
                - (i) For bills passed by Parliament, may:
                    - (i) Assent to the bill.
                    - (ii) Withhold assent.
                    - (iii) Return the bill (if not a money bill) for reconsideration. If repassed, the President must assent.
                - (j) For state bills reserved by the Governor, may:
                    - (i) Assent to the bill.
                    - (ii) Withhold assent.
                    - (iii) Direct the Governor to return the bill (if not a money bill) for reconsideration. The President is not obliged to assent even if repassed.
                - (k) Promulgates ordinances when Parliament is not in session, which must be approved within six weeks of reassembly or withdrawn.
                - (l) Lays reports of the Comptroller and Auditor General, Union Public Service Commission, Finance Commission, and others before Parliament.
                - (m) Makes regulations for the peace, progress, and good government of Andaman and Nicobar Islands, Lakshadweep, Dadra and Nagar Haveli, Daman and Diu, and Ladakh. For Puducherry, this applies when its assembly is suspended or dissolved.
        - Financial Powers
            - The President:
                - (a) Requires prior recommendation for money bills.
                - (b) Lays the annual financial statement (Union Budget) before Parliament.
                - (c) Ensures no demand for a grant is made without their recommendation.
                - (d) Makes advances from the Contingency Fund of India for unforeseen expenditure.
                - (e) Constitutes a Finance Commission every five years to recommend revenue distribution between Centre and states.
        - Judicial Powers
            - The President:
                - (a) Appoints the Chief Justice and judges of the Supreme Court and High Courts.
                - (b) May seek Supreme Court advice on questions of law or fact, though not bound by it.
                - (c) May grant pardons, reprieves, respites, remissions, or commute sentences for:
                    - (i) Court-martial punishments.
                    - (ii) Offences against Union laws.
                    - (iii) Death sentences.
        - Diplomatic Powers
            - International treaties and agreements are negotiated and concluded in the President’s name, subject to Parliamentary approval. The President represents India internationally and appoints and receives diplomats.
        - Military Powers
            - As supreme commander of the defence forces, the President appoints the chiefs of the Army, Navy, and Air Force and may declare war or peace, subject to Parliamentary approval.
        - Emergency Powers
            - The President may proclaim:
                - (a) National Emergency (Article 352) on grounds of war, external aggression, or armed rebellion, allowing modification of financial distribution and suspension of Fundamental Rights except Articles 20 and 21.
                - (b) President’s Rule (Articles 356 and 365) due to failure of state constitutional machinery or non-compliance with Union directions, enabling assumption of state functions, delegation of state legislative powers to Parliament, and authorization of state expenditure.
                - (c) Financial Emergency (Article 360) if India’s financial stability or credit is threatened, allowing salary reductions for Union employees, including judges.

    - Veto Power of the President
        - A bill passed by Parliament requires the President’s assent to become law. Under Article 111, the President may:
            - 1. Assent to the bill.
            - 2. Withhold assent.
            - 3. Return the bill (if not a money bill) for reconsideration. If repassed, the President must assent.
        - The veto power prevents hasty or unconstitutional legislation and includes:
            - 1. Absolute veto: Withholding assent, ending the bill, used for private members’ bills or government bills after cabinet changes.
            - 2. Suspensive veto: Returning a bill for reconsideration, overridden by repassage with an ordinary majority.
            - 3. Pocket veto: Taking no action, keeping the bill pending indefinitely.
        - Examples include:
            - In 1954, President Dr. Rajendra Prasad withheld assent to the PEPSU Appropriation Bill after President’s Rule was revoked.
            - In 1991, President R. Venkataraman withheld assent to the Salary, Allowances, and Pension of Members of Parliament (Amendment) Bill for lacking prior recommendation.
            - In 1986, President Zail Singh exercised pocket veto on the Indian Post Office (Amendment) Bill.
        - The President has no veto over constitutional amendment bills, per the 24th Constitutional Amendment Act of 1971.
        - For state legislation under Article 200, the Governor may:
            - 1. Assent to the bill.
            - 2. Withhold assent.
            - 3. Return the bill (if not a money bill) for reconsideration.
            - 4. Reserve the bill for the President’s consideration.
        - Under Article 201, the President may:
            - 1. Assent to the bill.
            - 2. Withhold assent.
            - 3. Direct the Governor to return the bill (if not a money bill). The President is not obliged to assent if repassed.
        - The President’s veto powers over Central and state legislation are summarized in:
            - ------------------------------------------------- Table 18.2 -------------------------------------------------

    - Ordinance-Making Power of the President
        - Under Article 123, the President may promulgate ordinances during Parliamentary recess, with the same force as acts of Parliament but temporary in nature. This power addresses urgent matters, subject to:
            - 1. Issuance only when both Houses are not in session or one House is not in session.
            - 2. The President’s satisfaction that immediate action is necessary, justiciable on grounds of malafide (Cooper vs. Union of India, 1970).
            - 3. Coextensive with Parliament’s law-making powers, subject to constitutional limitations, including Fundamental Rights.
            - 4. Ordinances must be laid before both Houses upon reassembly, ceasing to operate after six weeks if not approved or if disapproved earlier.
        - The President may withdraw ordinances at any time, acting on the Council of Ministers’ advice. Ordinances can be retrospective, modify or repeal acts, or amend tax laws but cannot amend the Constitution.
        - The Supreme Court in D.C. Wadhwa vs. State of Bihar (1986) ruled against successive repromulgation of ordinances without legislative approval, deeming it unconstitutional.
        - A statement explaining the need for an ordinance must accompany bills replacing it in the Lok Sabha.

    - Pardoning Power of the President
        - Under Article 72, the President may grant pardons, reprieves, respites, remissions, or commute sentences for:
            - 1. Offences against Union laws.
            - 2. Court-martial punishments.
            - 3. Death sentences.
        - This power, independent of the judiciary, corrects judicial errors or mitigates harsh sentences. It includes:
            - 1. Pardon: Absolves the convict from all sentences and disqualifications.
            - 2. Commutation: Substitutes a lighter punishment.
            - 3. Remission: Reduces the sentence period without changing its nature.
            - 4. Respite: Awards a lesser sentence due to special circumstances.
            - 5. Reprieve: Temporarily stays execution to allow time for pardon or commutation.
        - The Governor’s pardoning power under Article 161 differs as they cannot pardon court-martial sentences or death sentences, though they can suspend, remit, or commute death sentences concurrently with the President.
        - In Kehar Singh vs. Union of India (1988), the Supreme Court held that the President’s pardoning power is exercised on Cabinet advice, is not subject to oral hearings, and is justiciable only for arbitrariness or malafide.

    - Constitutional Position of the President
        - India’s parliamentary system makes the President a nominal executive, with real power vested in the Council of Ministers headed by the Prime Minister. The President acts on the Council’s advice, per Articles 53, 74, and 75.
        - The 42nd Constitutional Amendment Act of 1976 made the President’s adherence to ministerial advice binding. The 44th Amendment Act of 1978 allowed the President to return advice for reconsideration once, but the reconsidered advice is binding.
        - The President has situational discretion in:
            - (i) Appointing the Prime Minister when no party has a clear majority or after a sudden vacancy.
            - (ii) Dismissing the Council of Ministers if it loses Lok Sabha confidence.
            - (iii) Dissolving the Lok Sabha if the Council loses its majority.
        - Articles related to the President are summarized in:
            - ------------------------------------------------- Table 18.3 -------------------------------------------------


- CHAPTER 19: VICE-PRESIDENT
    - Introduction
        - The Vice-President occupies the second highest office in India, ranked next to the President in the official warrant of precedence.
        - The office is modelled on the American Vice-President, particularly in its role as the ex-officio Chairman of the Rajya Sabha.

    - Election
        - The Vice-President is elected indirectly by an electoral college consisting of both elected and nominated members of both Houses of Parliament.
            - The original Constitution required election by a joint meeting of both Houses, but this was amended by the 11th Constitutional Amendment Act of 1961 to simplify the process.
        - The electoral college differs from that for the President’s election in two respects:
            - 1. It includes both elected and nominated members of Parliament, whereas the President’s electoral college includes only elected members.
            - 2. It excludes members of state legislative assemblies, whereas the President’s electoral college includes elected members of state legislative assemblies.
        - Dr. B.R. Ambedkar explained this difference in the Constituent Assembly Debates (Volume VII, p. 1001):
            - The President’s powers extend to both Union and state administration, necessitating state legislature participation in their election.
            - The Vice-President’s primary role is to preside over the Rajya Sabha, with only temporary duties as acting President, making state legislature participation unnecessary.
        - The election follows the system of proportional representation by single transferable vote, with voting by secret ballot, similar to the President’s election.
            - A candidate must secure an absolute majority, calculated as: Electoral quota = (Total number of valid votes polled / 2) + 1.
            - Voters mark preferences (1, 2, 3, etc.), and votes are transferred from eliminated candidates until a candidate achieves the quota.
        - Disputes regarding the election are resolved by the Supreme Court, whose decision is final.
            - The election cannot be challenged due to an incomplete electoral college.
            - If an election is declared void, acts performed by the Vice-President before the declaration remain valid.
        - Vice-presidential elections from 1952 to 2022 are summarized in:
            - ------------------------------------------------- Table 19.1 -------------------------------------------------

    - Qualifications, Oath, and Conditions
        - Qualifications
            - To be eligible for election as Vice-President, a candidate must:
                - 1. Be a citizen of India.
                - 2. Have completed 35 years of age.
                - 3. Be qualified for election as a member of the Rajya Sabha.
                - 4. Not hold any office of profit under the Union, state, local, or public authority.
            - A sitting President, Vice-President, Governor, or Union/state minister is not deemed to hold an office of profit and is eligible.
            - Nominations require at least 20 electors as proposers and 20 as seconders, with a security deposit of Rs. 15,000 in the Reserve Bank of India, forfeitable if the candidate fails to secure one-sixth of the votes.
                - Per the Presidential and Vice-Presidential Elections Act, 1952, amended in 1997.
        - Oath or Affirmation
            - Before assuming office, the Vice-President swears to:
                - 1. Bear true faith and allegiance to the Constitution of India.
                - 2. Faithfully discharge the duties of the office.
            - The oath is administered by the President or a person appointed by them.
        - Conditions of Office
            - The Vice-President:
                - 1. Must not be a member of either House of Parliament or a state legislature; if elected, they are deemed to have vacated such a seat upon assuming office.
                - 2. Must not hold any other office of profit.

    - Term and Vacancy
        - Term of Office
            - The Vice-President serves a five-year term from the date of assuming office.
            - They may resign by addressing a letter to the President or be removed before their term ends.
            - Removal requires a resolution passed by a majority of all then members of the Rajya Sabha (effective majority) and agreed to by the Lok Sabha with a simple majority.
                - The resolution can only be introduced in the Rajya Sabha with at least 14 days’ notice.
                - No specific grounds for removal are mentioned in the Constitution.
            - The Vice-President can hold office beyond their term until a successor assumes charge and is eligible for re-election for any number of terms.
                - Dr. S. Radhakrishnan was elected for two terms (1952–1962).
        - Vacancy in Office
            - A vacancy arises due to:
                - 1. Expiry of the five-year term.
                - 2. Resignation.
                - 3. Removal.
                - 4. Death (e.g., Krishna Kant was the first Vice-President to die in office in 2002).
                - 5. Disqualification or void election.
            - Elections to fill a vacancy due to term expiry must occur before the term ends.
            - For vacancies due to resignation, removal, death, or otherwise, an election must be held as soon as possible, and the new Vice-President serves a full five-year term.

    - Powers and Functions
        - The Vice-President has two primary functions:
            - 1. Acts as the ex-officio Chairman of the Rajya Sabha, with powers and functions similar to the Speaker of the Lok Sabha, including presiding over sessions, maintaining order, and deciding on procedural matters.
            - 2. Acts as President or discharges presidential functions during a vacancy in the President’s office (due to resignation, impeachment, death, or otherwise) or when the President is unable to discharge duties due to absence, illness, or other causes.
                - The Vice-President can act as President for a maximum of six months, within which a new President must be elected.
                - Examples include:
                    - Vice-Presidents V.V. Giri and B.D. Jatti acted as President after the deaths of Presidents Dr. Zakir Hussain (1969) and Fakhruddin Ali Ahmed (1977), respectively.
                    - Vice-President Dr. S. Radhakrishnan discharged presidential functions in June 1960 and July 1961 due to President Dr. Rajendra Prasad’s absence and illness.
        - While acting as President, the Vice-President does not perform the duties of the Chairman of the Rajya Sabha, which are performed by the Deputy Chairman.
        - The Vice-President draws their salary as the ex-officio Chairman of the Rajya Sabha, increased in 2018 from Rs. 1.25 lakh to Rs. 4 lakh per month, effective January 1, 2016, per the Finance Act, 2018, amending the Salaries and Allowances of Officers of Parliament Act, 1953.
            - In 2008, the pension for retired Vice-Presidents was set at 50% of their salary, with additional benefits including a furnished residence, medical facilities, travel, and allowances, per the Vice-President’s Pension (Amendment) Act, 2008.
        - When acting as President, the Vice-President receives the President’s salary and allowances, not those of the Rajya Sabha Chairman.

    - Indian and American Vice-Presidents Compared
        - The Indian Vice-President’s office is modelled on the American Vice-President, particularly as the Chairman of the Senate (equivalent to the Rajya Sabha).
        - A key difference is that the American Vice-President succeeds to the presidency for the unexpired term upon a vacancy, while the Indian Vice-President only acts as President temporarily until a new President is elected.
        - The Indian Vice-President’s role is limited, leading some scholars to call them ‘His Superfluous Highness,’ but the office ensures political continuity.
        - Articles related to the Vice-President are summarized in:
            - ------------------------------------------------- Table 19.2 -------------------------------------------------


- CHAPTER 20: PRIME MINISTER
    - Introduction
        - In India’s parliamentary system, the President is the nominal executive (de jure), while the Prime Minister is the real executive (de facto).
        - The President is the head of the state, while the Prime Minister is the head of the government.

    - Appointment of the Prime Minister
        - The Constitution (Article 75) states that the Prime Minister is appointed by the President but does not specify the procedure.
        - Per parliamentary conventions, the President appoints the leader of the majority party or coalition in the Lok Sabha as the Prime Minister.
        - When no party has a clear majority, the President may exercise discretion, typically appointing the leader of the largest party or coalition and requiring a vote of confidence in the Lok Sabha within a specified period (e.g., one month).
            - Example: In 1979, President Neelam Sanjiva Reddy appointed Charan Singh as Prime Minister after the fall of the Janata Party government.
        - Discretion is also exercised when a Prime Minister dies suddenly without an obvious successor.
            - Example: In 1984, after Indira Gandhi’s assassination, President Zail Singh appointed Rajiv Gandhi, ignoring the precedent of a caretaker Prime Minister.
            - On the deaths of Jawaharlal Nehru (1964) and Lal Bahadur Shastri (1966), President appointed Gulzari Lal Nanda as interim Prime Minister until the Congress party elected a new leader.
        - If the ruling party elects a new leader after the Prime Minister’s death, the President must appoint them.
        - In 1980, the Delhi High Court ruled that a person need not prove majority support in the Lok Sabha before appointment; the President may appoint them and require proof of majority within a reasonable period.
            - Examples: Charan Singh (1979), V.P. Singh (1989), Chandrasekhar (1990), P.V. Narasimha Rao (1991), A.B. Vajpayee (1996, 1998), H.D. Deve Gowda (1996), and I.K. Gujral (1997).
        - In 1997, the Supreme Court ruled that a non-member of either House of Parliament can be appointed Prime Minister but must become a member within six months, failing which they cease to hold office.
        - The Prime Minister may be a member of either House of Parliament, unlike in Britain, where the Prime Minister must be from the House of Commons.
            - Examples: Indira Gandhi (1966), H.D. Deve Gowda (1996), and Manmohan Singh (2004) were Rajya Sabha members.

    - Oath, Term, and Salary
        - Before assuming office, the Prime Minister takes oaths of office and secrecy, administered by the President, similar to those for Union ministers.
        - In the oath of office, the Prime Minister swears to:
            - 1. Bear true faith and allegiance to the Constitution.
            - 2. Uphold India’s sovereignty and integrity.
            - 3. Faithfully and conscientiously discharge duties.
            - 4. Do right to all people per the Constitution and law, without fear, favour, affection, or ill will.
        - In the oath of secrecy, the Prime Minister swears not to reveal matters brought to their consideration except as required for official duties.
        - The Prime Minister’s term is not fixed and holds office during the President’s pleasure.
            - The President cannot dismiss the Prime Minister as long as they enjoy Lok Sabha majority support.
            - If they lose Lok Sabha confidence, they must resign, or the President may dismiss them.
                - Examples: V.P. Singh (1990) and H.D. Deve Gowda (1997) resigned after losing Lok Sabha confidence.
        - The salary and allowances are determined by Parliament, equivalent to a member of Parliament, plus a sumptuary allowance, free accommodation, travel, medical facilities, etc.
            - In 2001, the sumptuary allowance increased from Rs. 1,500 to Rs. 3,000 per month, per the Salaries and Allowances of Ministers (Amendment) Act, 2001.

    - Powers and Functions of the Prime Minister
        - In Relation to Council of Ministers
            - The Prime Minister, as head of the Council of Ministers, has the following powers:
                - 1. Recommends persons for appointment as ministers by the President, who can only appoint those recommended.
                - 2. Allocates and reshuffles portfolios among ministers.
                - 3. May ask a minister to resign or advise the President to dismiss them in case of differences.
                - 4. Presides over Council meetings and influences its decisions.
                - 5. Guides, directs, controls, and coordinates ministers’ activities.
                - 6. Can bring about the Council’s collapse by resigning, as the Council dissolves upon the Prime Minister’s resignation or death.
            - The resignation or death of any other minister creates a vacancy, which the Prime Minister may choose to fill.
        - In Relation to the President
            - The Prime Minister:
                - 1. Acts as the principal channel of communication between the President and the Council of Ministers (Article 78), with duties to:
                    - (a) Communicate all Council decisions on Union administration and legislative proposals.
                    - (b) Furnish information requested by the President.
                    - (c) Submit matters decided by a minister but not considered by the Council for its consideration if required by the President.
                - 2. Advises the President on appointments of key officials, including the Attorney General, Comptroller and Auditor General, UPSC Chairman and members, Election Commissioners, and Finance Commission Chairman and members.
        - In Relation to Parliament
            - As the leader of the Lok Sabha, the Prime Minister:
                - 1. Advises the President on summoning and proroguing Parliament sessions.
                - 2. May recommend Lok Sabha dissolution to the President.
                - 3. Announces government policies on the House floor.
        - Other Powers and Functions
            - The Prime Minister:
                - 1. Chairs bodies like NITI Aayog, National Integration Council, Inter-State Council, and National Water Resources Council.
                - 2. Shapes India’s foreign policy.
                - 3. Acts as the chief spokesperson of the Union government.
                - 4. Serves as the crisis manager-in-chief during emergencies.
                - 5. Engages with various sections of society, receiving memoranda on their issues.
                - 6. Leads the ruling party.
                - 7. Is the political head of the services.
            - Dr. B.R. Ambedkar noted that the Prime Minister, not the President, is comparable to the U.S. President in functional significance.

    - Role Descriptions
        - Eminent scholars’ descriptions of the British Prime Minister apply to the Indian context:
            - Lord Morley: ‘Primus inter pares’ (first among equals) and ‘keystone of the cabinet arch.’
            - Herbert Morrison: More than ‘primus inter pares,’ reflecting the Prime Minister’s dominant position.
            - Sir William Vernon Harcourt: ‘Inter stellas luna minores’ (a moon among lesser stars).
            - Jennings: A ‘sun around which planets revolve’ and ‘keystone of the constitution.’
            - H.J. Laski: Central to the cabinet’s formation, life, and death, the ‘pivot’ of governmental machinery.
            - H.R.G. Greaves: ‘Master of the Government.’
            - Munro: ‘Captain of the ship of the state.’
            - Ramsay Muir: ‘Steersman of the steering wheel of the ship of the state.’
        - Observers like R.H. Crossman and Humphrey Berkely describe the British system as ‘Prime Ministerial government,’ a term applicable to India due to the Prime Minister’s significant powers.

    - Relationship with the President
        - Constitutional provisions governing the relationship include:
            - 1. Article 74: The Council of Ministers, headed by the Prime Minister, aids and advises the President, who acts per their advice, though they may return advice for reconsideration once.
            - 2. Article 75: The President appoints the Prime Minister and other ministers on their advice; ministers hold office during the President’s pleasure, and the Council is collectively responsible to the Lok Sabha.
            - 3. Article 78: The Prime Minister’s duties include communicating Council decisions, furnishing information, and submitting matters for Council consideration as required by the President.

    - Chief Ministers Who Became Prime Ministers
        - Six individuals who were Chief Ministers before becoming Prime Ministers are:
            - 1. Morarji Desai: Bombay State (1952–1956), Prime Minister (1977–1979).
            - 2. Charan Singh: Uttar Pradesh (1967–1968, 1970), Prime Minister (1979–1980).
            - 3. V.P. Singh: Uttar Pradesh, Prime Minister (1989–1990).
            - 4. P.V. Narasimha Rao: Andhra Pradesh (1971–1973), Prime Minister (1991–1996).
            - 5. H.D. Deve Gowda: Karnataka, Prime Minister (1996–1997).
            - 6. Narendra Modi: Gujarat (2001–2014), Prime Minister (2014–present).

    - Caretaker Government
        - Meaning
            - The Constitution does not provide for a caretaker government; it is a situational arrangement.
            - A caretaker government:
                - 1. Manages routine activities after Lok Sabha dissolution until a new government is formed post-election, ensuring free and fair polls (J.C. Johari, *Indian Government and Policies*, 2001).
                - 2. Continues when a Council of Ministers resigns after losing Lok Sabha confidence but is asked to stay until alternative arrangements are made (Subhash C. Kashyap, *Our Constitution*, 2011).
        - Limited Role
            - A caretaker government handles routine administration, avoiding significant policy decisions or new initiatives except for national security or interest.
            - Key observations:
                - 1. The Tarkunde Committee (1974–1975) recommended that caretaker governments avoid new policies, projects, grants, or official functions attended by ministers.
                - 2. In 1979, President Neelam Sanjiva Reddy’s communique stated that the caretaker government would ensure fair elections and avoid new policies or significant spending.
                - 3. The Calcutta High Court (1979) noted that, despite no constitutional mention, a caretaker government is necessary for day-to-day administration until alternatives are arranged.
                - 4. Caretaker Prime Ministers are constrained by lack of Parliamentary accountability and the need to avoid electoral advantage (Rajeev Dhavan, *The Sunday Times of India*, 1999).
        - Articles related to the Prime Minister are summarized in:
            - ------------------------------------------------- Table 20.1 -------------------------------------------------


- CHAPTER 21: CENTRAL COUNCIL OF MINISTERS
    - Introduction
        - India’s parliamentary system, modelled on the British pattern, establishes the Council of Ministers, headed by the Prime Minister, as the real executive authority in the politico-administrative system.
        - The principles of the parliamentary system are not detailed in the Constitution, but Articles 74 and 75 outline the status, appointment, tenure, responsibility, qualifications, oaths, and salaries of ministers in a broad manner.

    - Constitutional Provisions
        - Article 74: Council of Ministers to Aid and Advise President
            - 1. There shall be a Council of Ministers, with the Prime Minister at its head, to aid and advise the President, who shall act in accordance with such advice. The President may require the Council to reconsider advice, and the President shall act per the reconsidered advice.
            - 2. The advice tendered by ministers to the President cannot be inquired into by any court.
        - Article 75: Other Provisions as to Ministers
            - 1. The Prime Minister is appointed by the President, and other ministers are appointed by the President on the Prime Minister’s advice.
            - 2. The total number of ministers, including the Prime Minister, shall not exceed 15% of the Lok Sabha’s total strength, per the 91st Constitutional Amendment Act of 2003.
            - 3. A member of either House of Parliament disqualified for defection is also disqualified from being appointed as a minister, per the 91st Constitutional Amendment Act of 2003.
            - 4. Ministers hold office during the pleasure of the President.
            - 5. The Council of Ministers is collectively responsible to the Lok Sabha.
            - 6. The President administers the oaths of office and secrecy to ministers.
            - 7. A minister who is not a member of either House of Parliament for six consecutive months ceases to be a minister.
            - 8. The salaries and allowances of ministers are determined by Parliament.
        - Article 77: Conduct of Business of the Government of India
            - 1. All executive actions of the Government of India are expressed in the President’s name.
            - 2. Orders and instruments made in the President’s name are authenticated per rules made by the President, and their validity cannot be questioned on grounds of not being made by the President.
            - 3. The President makes rules for the convenient transaction of government business and its allocation among ministers.
        - Article 78: Duties of Prime Minister
